# Benchmarks

Generated by `node bench/run.mjs`, except the JSON section at the end, which `node bench/json-compare/run.mjs --record` writes; do not edit by hand. The programs, the rules and the analysis of every gap are in [bench/README.md](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/bench/README.md) and [docs/wp9-optimisation.md](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/docs/wp9-optimisation.md).

String building used to hold every intermediate string until exit, because each `join` returns its result and the automatic arena scopes (WP6) cannot reclaim an escaping value; the call-site reclaim in `docs/wp9-optimisation.md` now frees a returned string's intermediates at the call.

## Data parallelism (ms, min / median)

Each kernel is one Nish binary, run as the loop a program writes without `nish/threads` (`seq`) and as `parallelMapInto` (`par`) on all 4 logical cores, par-histogram as a scope of four tasks sharing a `Mutex` and par-histogram-atomic as the same scope sharing an `AtomicI32Array` of the bins; the speedup is the loop's minimum over the map's. Both runs print the same checksum. The kernels are in `bench/par-*.ts`, and wp29 §8a reads the numbers. par-regions is what one divided region costs, and [wp20 §8e](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/docs/wp20-threads.md#8e-what-a-region-costs-and-why-it-is-not-a-goroutine) has it before and after the worker pool. This table alone was measured on 2026-11-14, on Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.10GHz, at `main` at f8e21091, the base of the branch it was measured on (branch commit c1d3f06f, which a squash merge does not keep), by `node bench/run.mjs --only par`; the rest of the file was measured on the machine in the header.

| Kernel | What | Loop | `parallelMapInto` | Speedup | Peak RSS loop / map (KB) |
| --- | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| par-compute | 64 square roots per element, 2^21 elements (f64) | 960 / 970 | 264 / 282 | 3.64x | 33,968 / 34,352 |
| par-alloc | a string formatted and summed per element, 2^22 elements; the body allocates (NL9012) | 210 / 219 | 60.0 / 63.6 | 3.50x | 33,956 / 34,520 |
| par-nbody | n-body partitioned: 1024 bodies, 16 steps, one map of 3n probes per step | 193 / 196 | 64.6 / 65.0 | 2.99x | 1,588 / 1,896 |
| par-short | an 8-element map called 2^20 times, each call fed by the last | 65.3 / 80.0 | 67.5 / 73.7 | 0.97x | 1,316 / 1,316 |
| par-regions | a 2^17-element map called 1,000 times, each call fed by the last: what a region costs | 630 / 669 | 60.4 / 66.0 | 10.44x | 2,212 / 2,776 |
| par-histogram | 4 tasks bin 50,000 values each into one `Mutex`, a lock per value, 16 rounds: a contended lock | 29.5 / 30.4 | 114 / 167 | 0.26x | 1,320 / 1,628 |
| par-histogram-atomic | par-histogram with the bins one `AtomicI32Array`, one `add` per value: contended atomics, no lock | 26.9 / 32.4 | 65.5 / 73.8 | 0.41x | 1,316 / 1,752 |
| par-heavy | 16 integer scramble rounds per element, 2^22 elements, 40 calls; the order-sensitive fold runs on one thread | 2608 / 2654 | 833 / 861 | 3.13x | 34,068 / 34,340 |
| par-heavy-fold | par-heavy with a fold that vectorises: the same map, a smaller undivided part | 2338 / 2508 | 806 / 844 | 2.90x | 34,072 / 34,340 |
| par-heavy4 | par-heavy with 64 scramble rounds: four times the divided work, the same fold | 12515 / 12691 | 3552 / 3750 | 3.52x | 34,072 / 34,524 |
| par-scale | `x * k` over 2^22 elements, 40 calls: a memory-bound map | 386 / 402 | 290 / 297 | 1.33x | 34,072 / 34,344 |
| par-single | one `x * 3` map over 2^25 elements: a single large region | 257 / 266 | 217 / 226 | 1.18x | 263,332 / 263,896 |

## Data parallelism against Rust and Go (ms, min / median)

Five of the kernels above, each ported to Rust and to Go with the same work, the same sizes and the same checksum: every binary below printed the Nish `par` run's output (numeric tokens to 1e-9 relative; the n-body energies agree to the last bit). The Rust twins run on `std::thread::scope` (`bench/par-<kernel>.rs`) and on Rayon's pool (`bench/par-<kernel>-rayon.rs`), the Go twins on goroutines and a `sync.WaitGroup` (`bench/par-<kernel>.go`). The map twins cut the array into one contiguous chunk per thread, the first on the calling thread, as Nish's partitioner did until it began cutting up to eight pieces per thread that the threads claim as they finish ([Scheduling a divided region](#scheduling-a-divided-region)); Rayon splits adaptively. par-histogram takes the lock once per value in every language, Nish's `Mutex`, Rust's `std::sync::Mutex` and Go's `sync.Mutex`, kept deliberately as a comparison of a contended lock. par-histogram-atomic bins the same values into an `AtomicI32` per bin, one sequentially consistent add per value in every language, Nish's `add`, Rust's `AtomicI32::fetch_add` with `SeqCst` and Go's `atomic.Int32.Add`: the same contention on sixteen bins without the lock. The version that avoids the contention, a local tally and one `add` per bin, is `examples/histogram-atomic.ts` ([the threads guide's atomics section](guide/using-nish/threads.md#counting-without-a-lock-atomici32-and-atomici64)).

**The same work, not the same checks or the same build.** Nish builds with its default checks: every integer operation that can overflow and every index it cannot prove in range is checked (`nish_panic_overflow` and `nish_panic_index` are reached from par-histogram's `binStream`, for one). The Rust twins build as `--release` does, with bounds checks and without overflow checks; Go checks bounds and not overflow. And Nish's `--profile speed` links with LTO, where neither Rust build does, which gives Nish a small edge. In par-nbody each Nish probe reaches the bodies through class references, where the twins index a contiguous array of structs, which slightly favours Rust and Go.

**How it is timed.** Every binary divides over 4 threads, the count Nish's runtime chooses on this machine: `NISH_NUM_THREADS`, `RAYON_NUM_THREADS`, `GOMAXPROCS` and `PAR_THREADS` (read by the scope twins) are all set to it. The comparison is run 3 time(s), minutes apart, each run 11 interleaved rounds after 1 warm-up: in each round every binary and a byte-identical copy of it run once, in an order rotated by one per round. The times below are process wall time, min / median in ms over every round of every run. In each run, and separately for the minimum and the median, Nish is divided by the fastest other column on that statistic, and the band is the larger of the gaps between Nish and its copy and between that column and its copy. A ratio reads as faster or slower only outside the band, and within 0.5% of its edge as at the edge. The reading takes every run together: runs that fall on both sides of the band make no claim. The Nish column is the `par` run of the table above, timed again here beside seven other binaries rather than in that table's alternation with its loop, so the two tables' Nish figures differ by about as much as this machine moves between runs: compare within a table, not across.

Measured on 2026-11-14, on Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.10GHz, 4 logical cores, 4 threads, linux 6.18.44-fc-v470, by `node bench/run.mjs --only par` at `main` at f8e21091, the base of the branch it was measured on (branch commit c1d3f06f, which a squash merge does not keep). nish 0.39.0; rustc 1.97.0 (2d8144b78 2026-07-07); Rayon 1.12.0, built by cargo with the same profile; go version go1.24.7 linux/amd64.

## Scheduling a divided region

Measured on 2026-10-20 on the machine of the data-parallelism table above (Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.10GHz, 4 logical cores), every figure a median in ms over interleaved rounds in which each binary and a byte-identical copy of it ran once; the copy's gap is the noise band, and a difference inside it is no difference.

`nish_parallel_range` used to cut a divided region into one contiguous chunk per thread. A thread that was preempted held every other thread at the join. It now cuts the range into up to eight pieces per thread, never smaller than the grain, and each thread claims the next piece with one relaxed `fetch_add` as it finishes the last; the calling thread runs the first. Eight is the flat middle of a sweep over par-heavy at four threads, nine rounds: 2 pieces per thread was slower and noisier (868, 766–1121), 4 to 32 lay within the copy band (805–822), and so did par-scale's memory-bound map (262–270).

| Kernel | What | `main` | copy | this runtime | copy |
| --- | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| par-heavy, 4 threads, 15 rounds | 16 scramble rounds per element, 2^22 elements, 40 calls | 846 | 834 | 792 | 771 |
| par-scale, 4 threads | `x * k`, 2^22 elements, 40 calls: memory-bound | 277 | 266 | 270 | 260 |
| par-single, 4 threads | one `x * 3` map over 2^25 elements | 213 | 209 | 213 | 207 |
| par-alloc, 4 threads, 9 rounds | a string formatted per element, 2^22 elements: the body allocates | 88 | 79 | 52 | 51 |
| 3 chunks above the grain | par-heavy at 600,000 elements, 300 calls: three chunks of the grain | 1053 | 1036 | 1071 | 1174 |
| 8 chunks above the grain | par-heavy at 2^21 elements, 80 calls: eight chunks | 843 | 781 | 815 | 787 |

par-heavy at one thread is 2172 (2108–2315), within main's band. Over that one-thread loop, on the medians of the same rounds, par-heavy goes from about 2.6x on `main` (2172 / 846) to about 2.7x (2172 / 792), about 6% faster at four threads and outside the copy band (846 / 834 against 792 / 771); the data-parallelism table above, on minima, has 2.91x. par-alloc, whose body allocates, is the largest gain: 79–88 ms on `main`, 51–52 here, and the gain is the pieces' own: the same runtime with the arena reset between pieces taken out measures 53, the same. par-histogram is a scope of tasks, not a divided region, and its contended lock spreads each binary over 89–258 ms; its medians, 223–229 on `main` and 199–206 here over 15 rounds, are no difference. The others are unchanged.

A single 2^25 map is not slower than at efbbd8c8 (212 / 211) or on `main`, and workers touching their own pages first (first-touch) measured within the band, so it is not shipped.

**par-heavy does not reach 3x, and the scheduler is not why.** Its checksum is an order-sensitive fold over the result, which runs on one thread, and so do the fill and the allocation before the map. Timed with zero scramble rounds, so that the map does nearly nothing, the program takes 252 with the fold and 75 without it: the fold is about 177 ms and the fill, the allocation and the near-empty map about 75, some 286 ms of the 2172 one-thread run (13%).

By Amdahl the best four cores can do is (2172 − 286) / 4 + 286 ≈ 757 ms, a cap of about 2.9x; the program measures about 2.7x on these medians. Inside the region each thread was busy for 500–544 ms of an ideal 471 (87–93% busy efficiency), the region's wall time was 555–591 ms (80–85%), threads started within 1.6–5 ms of each other and finished within 30–60 ms, and each claimed 152–171 pieces.

The fold, not the scheduler, explains `main`'s 2.6x. With the serial part made smaller or the divided part larger, the same map clears 3x on both runtimes: par-heavy-fold, whose fold vectorises, measures about 3.15x, and par-heavy4, with four times the scramble rounds and the same fold, about 3.4x.

## Map and Set (WP32)

Each workload is timed by the program around the workload alone, not as process wall time, and every column is its own process, the columns taking turns round by round: 5 timed runs after 1 warm-up, min / median in ms. The workloads are [docs/wp32-map.md](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/docs/wp32-map.md) §2's, over the same keys, and every program prints checksum lines `bench/map-node.mjs` prints from Node's `Map`. `unordered` is the S1 prototype with keys and values in the buckets, the layout the language does not ship; the ratios divide minimums. The programs are built with `nish bench/<name>.ts --link <exe> --profile speed`, and bench/README.md describes them; §10 of the note reads the numbers.

```
nish build/bench/src/fib.ts --link build/bench/fib-nish --profile speed
nish build/bench/src/fib.ts --link build/bench/fib-nish-size --profile size
clang -O3 -s build/bench/src/fib.c -lm -o build/bench/fib-c
rustc -C opt-level=3 -C panic=abort -C codegen-units=1 -C strip=symbols build/bench/src/fib.rs -o build/bench/fib-rust
rustc -C opt-level=3 -C panic=abort -C codegen-units=1 -C strip=symbols -C target-cpu=native build/bench/src/fib.rs -o build/bench/fib-rust-native
go build -trimpath -ldflags=-s -w -o build/bench/fib-go build/bench/src/fib.go
```

Go has no optimisation level to choose: `-trimpath` and `-ldflags=-s -w` only strip the binary. `nish --link` runs `scripts/build.sh --profile speed` (`clang -O3 -flto` with section GC and stripping; see the README) over the module and `runtime/runtime.c`.

<!-- json-compare:begin -->
## JSON against other languages

Written by `node bench/json-compare/run.mjs --record`, not by `bench/run.mjs`, which carries this section over when it rewrites the file. The run is its own, so the host below may not be the one in the table at the head of this file. The workloads, the checksums and the measurement rules are in [that script's header](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/bench/json-compare/run.mjs), and the Nish program is [main.ts](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/bench/json-compare/main.ts).

The ratio column divides the Nish minimum by that implementation's minimum, as the tables above do: above 1.00x it is faster than Nish. A ratio within the noise band, the gap between Nish and a byte-identical copy of its binary, is marked as no difference.

Builds: Nish `--link --profile speed`; C `clang -O3 -DNDEBUG` and C++ `clang++ -O3 -DNDEBUG`; serde_json cargo `--release` with LTO and one codegen unit; Go `go build`.

What differs between the rows:

- **Tokenize** is each library's own way of visiting every value. Nish's tokenizer copies every key and string out with `stringInto`; yyjson builds a whole tree, reading a copy of the input rather than in place; RapidJSON's Reader (SAX) calls back per value; serde_json drives a Visitor; Go's `Decoder.Token` makes an `interface{}` per token. simdjson On-Demand materialises every value as it is visited. What must agree: in the timed loop, each token's kind and each key's and string's length and last byte, folded, and the sum of the numbers as a double; in an untimed pass over the last iteration's input, every byte of every key and string.
- **Typed decode** has no yyjson or RapidJSON row: neither has a typed layer. Every row decodes into a fresh value each iteration. simdjson On-Demand reads `performances` and nothing after it, while serde_json, Go and Nish scan the whole document, so simdjson's MB/s divides the whole document's bytes by a time that did not read all of them. The extra Nish row decodes into one value reused across iterations, which nish/json-typed supports and the others' APIs do not. What must agree: every decoded field of every performance, each string byte by byte, folded each iteration.
- **Serialize** times the writing alone, from the same decoded values. Nish (`jsonEncode`) and serde_json (derive) write from the type, Go by reflection (its encoder's trailing newline is trimmed), and simdjson's row is builder calls written by hand, field by field, since simdjson has no type-driven writer without C++26 reflection. What must agree is a 64-bit `h * 31 + v` fold of every byte of every iteration's output, and the last output's length.

| Date | 2026-11-14 |
| nish | nish 0.39.0 (commit ae459f3a) |
| CPU | Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.30GHz, 4 logical cores |
| OS | linux 6.18.44-fc-v470 (x64) |
| Toolchains | Nish: nish 0.39.0; C++: `clang++` Ubuntu clang version 18.1.3 (1ubuntu1); C: `clang` Ubuntu clang version 18.1.3 (1ubuntu1); Rust: cargo 1.97.0 (c980f4866 2026-06-30), rustc 1.97.0 (2d8144b78 2026-07-07); Go: `go` go version go1.24.7 linux/amd64 |
| Command | `npm run build && node bench/json-compare/run.mjs --rounds 9 --mb 128 --record` |
| Runs | interleaved rounds, at least 9 and for each case the next multiple of twice its implementations (the round count is in each heading), 128 MB of input per run; ns per document, min and median over the rounds |

### Held-out payloads

Instructions per document (callgrind, `bench/json-compare/main.ts tokenize --profile speed`), the tokenizer before the JSON tokenize-gap work against after it. The three payloads are not in the corpus the work was tuned on: `metrics` (about 1 MB of numbers) and `api` (about 1 MB of records with some non-ASCII text) come from a seeded generator, and `small` is the 242-byte body of `bench/json-tokenizer`. Their SHA-256 hashes are pinned in `bench/json-compare/heldout.mjs`, which also writes them. The base is `f8e21091`, built by `scripts/bootstrap.sh` in a worktree of that commit; current is `fb4b625e` (#1588, #1590 and #1600 merged). The record above was taken at `ae459f3a`, which is `fb4b625e` plus `bench/json-compare/heldout.mjs` and nothing else, so it measures the same compiler, `std/` and runtime. Both compile the same `main.ts` with `--profile speed`. The checksum line is equal for every pair, so both tokenize the same tokens. The base build's `src/` differs from main's by about 20 lines in three files.

| Payload | Base Ir/doc | Current Ir/doc | Change |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| metrics | 55,219,452 | 43,961,649 | -20.4% |
| api | 20,944,037 | 17,410,464 | -16.9% |
| small | 12,794 | 11,952 | -6.6% |

Reproduce: `node bench/json-compare/heldout.mjs --base <base build>/build/nish`. No comparator rows are missing from the record above: simdjson, yyjson, RapidJSON, serde_json and Go all ran.

<!-- json-writer-bytes:begin -->
### JsonWriter's byte writers

`JsonWriter.keyBytes` and `stringBytes` copy runs of plain bytes with the `skipJsonPlain` kernel (`nish/bytes`) from 16 bytes up. `jsonEncode` writes `string`s through `jsonWriterPutString`, which did not change, so the serialize table above does not measure this: in an A/B against the base, `804120e`, serialize on citm ran 0.988x at the minimum, 0.9 points past that run's 0.3% band (the branch against its byte-identical copy). Re-run against `3dca351` with the class kernels' loops restored to main's instructions, it ran 0.988x again, inside that run's 1.4% band. In the same run (12 rounds, minimums, each band the branch against its byte-identical copy), tokenize ran 1.025x on twitter (band 0.7%), 0.994x on citm (0.5%) and 1.048x on canada (0.3%), typed decode on citm 1.003x (0.9%) and typed-reuse 0.955x (4.2%), and `bench/json-tokenizer`'s 242-byte body ran 0.99x, inside its 0.6% band.

Two rows are past their bands: tokenize on citm by 0.1 points and typed-reuse by 0.3 points. Both run machine code identical to main's — the class paths' instructions are main's, and neither workload writes — and in typed-reuse the branch's byte-identical copy ran within 0.3% of the base, so the copy band is the evidence for reading both as noise; none of these gaps, faster or slower, is claimed.

`JsonDocument.write`, the in-tree caller of the byte writers, ran **1.49x** faster on twitter.json and 1.03x to 1.07x on citm_catalog.json at the minimum (12 and 10 interleaved rounds; bands against a byte-identical copy 0.4% and 2.7%). canada.json also ran faster, but a build with the kernel switched off did as well, so that gain is code shape, not the kernel.

On citm, the escape scan does not make serialize faster outside its band: most strings are shorter than 16 bytes, and its ceiling at the 16-byte guard, 1.0139x, is inside the 1.4% band by 0.01 points. The profile, the guard and the full A/B are in [wp38-simd.md](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/docs/wp38-simd.md), *JsonWriter's byte writers*.
<!-- json-writer-bytes:end -->

<!-- json-encode-keys:begin -->
### `jsonEncode`'s keys, one pre-escaped copy

`jsonEncode` of a class writes each key with `JsonWriter.keyQuoted`: the compiler quotes and escapes the key once, at compile time, into a string constant (`"name":`), and the writer copies it in one go after the comma and indent. Before, every key went through the same escape scan as a runtime string. `JsonWriter.i32` and `i64` also count their digits by compares rather than divides. The output is byte-identical.

Instructions per document under callgrind: citm_catalog.json 14.13M → 10.74M (−24.0%); `bench/json-api`'s page of twenty users 188,879 → 162,647 (−13.9%). After the change, `keyQuoted` is about a quarter of either profile, `JsonWriter.i64` 31% of citm's, and `jsonWriterPutString` (escaping the string values) a third of the API page's.

Wall clock, against main at `f8e21091`. In `bench/json-compare`, serialize on citm ran 548,213 and 554,908 ns/doc at the minimum in two runs, against main's 767,648 (**1.38x to 1.40x** at the minimum). The bands, each the build against its byte-identical copy on the minimums, were 8.8%, 5.8% and 0.2%. In `node bench/json-api/run.mjs --base <main's nish>` (18 interleaved rounds), the page took 8,676 against 11,149 ns at the minimum (**1.29x**) and 9,710 against 11,856 ns at the median (**1.22x**); the noise band, main against its own copy, was 2.3% on the minimums and 1.6% on the medians.

#### Main's profile before the change, and the re-measure

The claim was that the escape scan of each key and the writer's per-key state checks were the largest cost per field in typed serialize. The by-function profile of main just before the change, `d822c60d` (`e966fbe5^`), confirms it on both workloads. On citm_catalog.json key writing is 35.0% of the instructions per document, against `JsonWriter.i64`'s 30.5%, a close second. On `bench/json-api`'s page it is 33.1%, against string values' 30.9%. The escape scan is most of it: 30.7% of citm's total and 25.8% of the page's. The state checks, with the comma and colon bytes that `key` writes, are the rest: 4.3% and 7.3%.

Each group's Ir/doc and share of the whole run. The share of the run includes the harness's checksum fold. The fold runs over the output after the timed region and is the same code in both builds.

| citm_catalog.json | Before (`d822c60d`) | Share | After (`e966fbe5`) | Share |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Key writing: escape scan (`jsonWriterPutString` called for keys) | 4,335,399 | 30.73% | — | — |
| Key writing: state checks and separators (`JsonWriter.key` self plus its lines inlined in the loop) | 601,146 | 4.26% | — | — |
| Key writing: `JsonWriter.keyQuoted` | — | — | 2,704,943 | 25.31% |
| Numbers: `JsonWriter.i64` (digits inlined) | 4,301,426 | 30.49% | 3,321,628 | 31.08% |
| String values: `JsonWriter.string`, `jsonEncodeStringOrNull` | 173,583 | 1.23% | 173,583 | 1.62% |
| Checksum fold (harness, outside the clock) | 2,602,071 | 18.44% | 2,602,070 | 24.34% |
| Rest: brackets, value checks, the generated encoder, inlined byte writes | 2,094,487 | 14.85% | 1,886,323 | 17.65% |
| Total | 14,108,112 | 100% | 10,688,547 | 100% |

| `bench/json-api` page | Before (`d822c60d`) | Share | After (`e966fbe5`) | Share |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Key writing: escape scan (`jsonWriterPutString` called from `key`) | 48,620 | 25.77% | — | — |
| Key writing: state checks and separators (`JsonWriter.key` self) | 13,852 | 7.34% | — | — |
| Key writing: `JsonWriter.keyQuoted` | — | — | 38,143 | 23.52% |
| String values: `JsonWriter.string` inclusive | 58,342 | 30.92% | 58,342 | 35.98% |
| Numbers: `JsonWriter.i32` and `i64` | 10,721 | 5.68% | 8,188 | 5.05% |
| Checksum fold (harness, outside the clock) | 39,951 | 21.17% | 39,951 | 24.64% |
| Rest: the generated encoder, `boolean`, brackets, `memcpy` | 17,219 | 9.12% | 17,545 | 10.82% |
| Total | 188,705 | 100% | 162,169 | 100% |

Shares are rounded, so the after column for the page sums to 100.01%. Without the fold, key writing was 42.9% of citm's encode and 42.0% of the page's. Key writing is a lower bound on citm. The byte writes the loop inlines are shared with values and brackets and are counted in "Rest".

Every key went through the scan. Per citm document, the before build calls `jsonWriterPutString` 24,444 times: 17,370 calls from `key` inlined in the loop, 6,723 from the out-of-line `JsonWriter.key`, and 351 from `JsonWriter.string`. The after build calls `keyQuoted` 24,093 times (17,370 + 6,723) and `jsonWriterPutString` only from `string`. The page writes 326 keys and 207 string values, and the after build never takes `keyQuoted`'s fallback to `key`.

The shares are from builds with `-g`, so that inlined lines carry their function's name. Their Ir/doc is within 0.5% of the stripped builds (−0.18%, −0.47%, −0.09% and −0.29%). The Ir/doc figures below are from the stripped builds, re-measured in this run against #1592's:

| Workload | Build | Totals (callgrind) | Ir/doc | #1592 | Difference |
| --- | --- | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| citm | `d822c60d` | N=1 43,038,361; N=10 170,241,812 | 14,133,717 | 14.13M | +0.03% |
| citm | `e966fbe5` | N=1 39,643,444; N=10 136,291,985 | 10,738,727 | 10.74M | −0.01% |
| json-api | `d822c60d` | N=200 38,120,381; N=2000 378,102,615 | 188,879 | 188,879 | 0 |
| json-api | `e966fbe5` | N=200 32,873,981; N=2000 325,638,601 | 162,647 | 162,647 | 0 |

The change is −24.02% on citm and −13.89% on the page, which matches #1592. The difference columns compare #1592's rounded figures. Ir/doc is (Ir(N_hi) − Ir(N_lo)) / (N_hi − N_lo), the method of `heldout.mjs`.

Both builds print the same serialize checksum on citm at 297 iterations, the bench's count for a 452,530-byte document: `serialize 18100868296888812225 452530`. The page's checksum line is also equal between builds at 200, 1000 and 2000 iterations (`checksum 15878354662562373591 bytes 6931` at 1000).

Toolchain: x86_64 (Xeon @ 2.10GHz), valgrind-3.22.0, Ubuntu clang/LLVM 18.1.3, node v22.23.4. Each compiler was built by `scripts/bootstrap.sh` from seed 0.39.0 in a worktree of its commit. Both builds compile `bench/json-api/main.ts` as it is at `e966fbe5`, which is identical to main's. Reproduce, in each worktree:

```sh
node bench/json-compare/fetch.mjs   # the corpus
./build/nish bench/json-compare/main.ts -o out/ --link citm-main --profile speed   # add -g for the by-function shares
./build/nish <e966fbe5's bench/json-api/main.ts> -o out/ --link api-main --profile speed
valgrind --tool=callgrind --callgrind-out-file=citm-N.out out/citm-main serialize bench/json-compare/cache/corpus/citm_catalog.json N -1   # N = 1, 10; 297 for the checksum
valgrind --tool=callgrind --callgrind-out-file=api-N.out out/api-main N                                                              # N = 200, 2000
callgrind_annotate --inclusive=yes citm-10.out    # and --tree=caller for call counts
```
<!-- json-encode-keys:end -->

<!-- json-value-ranges:begin -->

### JsonDocument's source ranges

`JsonDocument.parseRanged` and `parseTextRanged` record each value's and each member key's byte range (about 20 bytes more a value); `parse` and `parseText` record none. Retired instructions under cachegrind, from one run of `node bench/json-value/measure.mjs --instructions --before <main's nish>` against main at `1391cf8`, in `bench/run.mjs`'s pinned environment (fixed libc tunables, `NISH_SIMD=base`, a fixed hash key), 16 MB of input a case. `head` is this tree's plain `parse` loop, `before` main's build of the same program, `on` the same loop calling `parseRanged`, and `both` one `parseRanged` followed by the plain `parse` loop in one binary. Every binary is also counted as a byte-identical copy; the band is the gap between the two counts.

| Row                                        |        before |          head | head − before | head / before | on / head | both / head |   band |
| ------------------------------------------ | ------------: | ------------: | ------------: | ------------: | --------: | ----------: | -----: |
| parse twitter.json (27 parses)             |   753,801,566 |   753,801,541 |           −25 |       −0.000% |   +4.089% |     +3.749% | 0.000% |
| parse citm_catalog.json (10 parses)        |   411,471,288 |   411,471,271 |           −17 |       −0.000% |   +9.427% |    +10.998% | 0.000% |
| parse canada.json (7 parses)               | 1,015,203,067 | 1,015,203,048 |           −19 |       −0.000% |  +10.416% |    +16.239% | 0.000% |
| parse sample.json, 1,427 bytes (11,757)    | 1,091,940,938 | 1,091,929,215 |       −11,723 |       −0.001% |   +7.603% |     −0.146% | 0.000% |
| builders (500 documents of 1,100 values)   |   882,996,248 |   881,763,264 |    −1,232,984 |       −0.140% |           |             | 0.000% |
| json-compare tokenize twitter.json         |   353,787,664 |   353,787,664 |             0 |       +0.000% |           |             | 0.000% |
| json-compare tokenize citm_catalog.json    |   297,763,836 |   297,763,822 |           −14 |       −0.000% |           |             | 0.000% |
| json-compare tokenize canada.json          |   993,834,293 |   993,834,293 |             0 |       +0.000% |           |             | 0.000% |
| json-compare typed                         |   306,561,355 |   306,561,383 |           +28 |       +0.000% |           |             | 0.000% |
| json-compare typed-reuse                   |   303,863,883 |   303,863,883 |             0 |       +0.000% |           |             | 0.000% |
| http1-server `/json-echo-doc`, 2,000 POSTs |   161,803,094 |   161,801,102 |        −1,992 |       −0.001% |           |             | 0.000% |

No row retires more than main's but typed, by 28 instructions in 306 million, which is inside what the two byte-identical copies of the same binary differ by in this run (`head-copy` counts 1 more, `before-copy` 29 more); the tokenize and typed binaries do not reach `nish/json-value`. A plain `parse` writes no range and resets none: ranges carry the generation they were written in and the readers answer `-1` for any other, so `clear` steps the generation as main's does, with one store more, of the range stamp, on the one clear in 511 that wraps it; and a document that never keeps ranges never makes their arrays.

`parseRanged` costs 4.1% to 10.4% more than `parse` on the four documents. A binary that links both entry points (`both`) pays for the second in its plain `parse` loop, 3.7% to 16.2% on the large documents and −0.15% on sample.json, from layout and inlining rather than noise: every band is 0.000%.

`parseRanged` keeps its own copies of `storeSource`, `duplicateKeys` and `sortPending`, so that a second caller does not keep them from inlining into `parse`. `append`, which every builder goes through, moves a full container's key ranges only when the slots have some of this generation, and reads the range arrays directly there, so that the move inlined into `append` keeps its not-full path straight. No timings are recorded: unpinned wall-clock bands on this machine ran 2% to 8%, wider than every gap above.

<!-- json-value-ranges:end -->

<!-- techempower:begin -->
## TechEmpower json and plaintext

Measured by `bash bench/techempower/run.sh` (3 interleaved rounds of 10 s, every contestant started afresh and warmed for 1 s before each count), and copied here from the report it writes to `build/tfb/results.md`. `bench/run.mjs` carries this section over between its `techempower` markers when it rewrites the file. The entry is [bench/techempower/](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/bench/techempower/README.md): one `pollWait` loop on `Http1Server`, run here as one process: pinned to one CPU, it starts one worker (the multi-core figures are [below](#one-process-against-one-worker-a-core-wp39-e5)). The other rows are the HTTP twins of [bench/http-twins/](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/bench/http-twins/README.md), each run as its own `serve <port>`.

`entry-copy` is a second build of the same `main.ts` that `cmp` finds byte-identical, so the gap between `entry` and `entry-copy` is the noise band every other difference is read against. `entry-nodelay0` is `entry` started with its `nodelay` argument 0, so its accepted sockets keep Nagle on. A cell marked `*` had non-2xx answers or socket errors in at least one round; `n/a` is a cell no round gave a figure for.

| | |
| --- | --- |
| Date | 2026-10-21 |
| nish | nish 0.18.0 (commit 5ee6e7c), `--link --profile speed` |
| CPU | Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.80GHz, 4 logical cores |
| OS | linux 6.18.44-fc-v139 (x64) |
| Client | wrk 4.1.0, `-t 3`, TFB's request headers, `--timeout 8`; `/plaintext` through `bench/techempower/pipeline.lua` 16 deep |
| Pinning | server on CPU 0, wrk on CPUs 1-3. **Client-shared**: wrk and the server share one 4-core machine, so three cores drive one server core, and a server faster than wrk can drive shows wrk's ceiling |
| Limits | `ulimit -n` 20000, `net.core.somaxconn` 4096; no concurrency was clamped |
| Command | `npm run build && bash bench/http-twins/build.sh && bash bench/techempower/run.sh --nodelay-compare` |

What the rows say, read against that band:

- The twins answer `/json` with `{"message":"Hello, World!","n":<n>}`: 8 bytes more than the entry's `{"message":"Hello, World!"}`, and a counter. The entry serializes its body with a `JsonWriter` on every request, as TFB requires; the twins vary in how they build theirs.
- One core of the entry serves `/plaintext` at medians of 290,467 to 324,456 req/s across every concurrency: below `uws` everywhere, below `go-fasthttp` from 256 to 4096 connections, and above every other twin. At 16384 `go-fasthttp` falls to 264,089, 9.1% under the entry's 290,467 and outside that level's 0.4% band.
- On `/json` the entry leads every twin at every concurrency by more than the band. Its narrowest lead is over `uws` at 16 connections, 62,677 against 55,513 (11.4%, where the band is 1.4%); the widest band, 6.0% at 512, is under its 12.0% lead there (80,449 against 70,785).
- At 16384 connections wrk reported read errors (connections reset) against every Nish build: the entry in two of its three rounds (250 and 2,452), the copy in two (2,916 and 1,517), and `entry-nodelay0` in two (2,075 and 4,418). Several twins had read errors or timeouts there too, and the `*` marks them.
- `rust-actix` answers about 10,000 req/s up to 1024 connections, falls to 5,632 at 4096 with timeouts in every round, and has no figure at 16384: in all three rounds wrk reported 0.00 req/s and no socket errors, so the cell is `n/a`. An earlier run of this section recorded that cell as 0, which was the failed measurement rather than a rate. Run alone, the twin shows 6.4 ms of latency per request too, so its figures are its own, reported as measured.
- `TCP_NODELAY` (WP39 E2, `tcpListenFlags` flag 2) is the entry's default. Against `entry-nodelay0` it is faster outside the band at four levels (`/plaintext` 256 connections by 2.0% against a 1.0% band, and 4096 by 0.9% against 0.7%; `/json` 128 by 7.5% against 0.1%, and 256 by 8.2% against 0.2%) and within the band at the other six, slower outside it at none. So the flag stays on.
- The benchmarked build is `5ee6e7c`. The head adds only the listener's pause when an accept runs out of descriptors, which this run never reached: with `ulimit -n` at 20000, no accept failed for want of one.

### One process against one worker a core (WP39 E5)

`main.ts` takes a fifth argument, its workers: 0, the default, runs one worker a CPU under `nish/net/prefork` on one `SO_REUSEPORT` port; 1 runs the one process the tables above measure. `run.sh --cores-compare` times both from the same build. `entry` and `entry-copy` are the byte-identical pair started with workers 1, and so give the noise band. `entry-cores` is `entry` started with workers 0. The server gets CPUs 0-1, so `entry-cores` runs two pinned workers, and wrk gets CPUs 2-3. These figures are not comparable with the one-core tables above: they come from a different machine, build and pinning.

| CPU | Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.10GHz, 4 logical cores (a cloud VM) |
| OS | linux 6.18.44-fc-v378 (x64) |
| Client | wrk 4.1.0, `-t 2`, TFB's request headers, `--timeout 8`; `/plaintext` through `pipeline.lua` 16 deep |
| Pinning | server on CPUs 0-1, wrk on CPUs 2-3. **Client-shared**: two wrk cores drive two server cores |
| Limits | `ulimit -n` 20000, `net.core.somaxconn` 4096; no concurrency was clamped |
| Command | `npm run build && bash bench/techempower/run.sh --cores-compare` |

| contestant | 256 | 1024 | 4096 | 16384 |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| entry | 1359317 (1309460) | 1170871 (901448) | 964963 (941896) | 888618 (786206)* |

| entry-cores | 2716939 (2684038) | 2570961 (2524748) | 2253033 (2192759) | 1689248 (1649006) |

Noise band (medians): 5.3%, 5.5%, 3.2%, 14.8%. `entry-cores` against `entry`: +99.9%, +119.6%, +133.5%, +90.1%, each outside its band.

`/json`, req/s, median (min) of 3 rounds of 10 s:

| contestant | 16 | 32 | 64 | 128 | 256 | 512 |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| entry | 128488 (124765) | 141208 (138229) | 155871 (145938) | 160087 (157302) | 167444 (167011) | 196739 (185709) |
| entry-copy | 126916 (124397) | 141923 (139049) | 152071 (141611) | 157385 (149838) | 170613 (169851) | 191569 (172509) |
| entry-cores | 248324 (240201) | 272422 (258128) | 284092 (272414) | 306074 (299083) | 321831 (318143) | 341548 (328258) |

Noise band (medians): 1.2%, 0.5%, 2.4%, 1.7%, 1.9%, 2.6%. `entry-cores` against `entry`: +93.3%, +92.9%, +82.3%, +91.2%, +92.2%, +73.6%, each outside its band.

- Two workers on two cores serve 1.7 to 2.3 times the one process at every concurrency. A gain above 2x, as on `/plaintext` at 1024 and 4096, is not explained by these runs. One unmeasured hypothesis: the one process is not pinned inside its two CPUs, so it pays for migrations the pinned workers do not.
- The gain shrinks at the top concurrencies: 16384 on `/plaintext` and 512 on `/json`. Two wrk cores are likely the ceiling there, since this machine is client-shared.
- At `/plaintext` 16384, `entry` had 4,679 read errors (connections reset) in one round, which the `*` marks. The 14.8% band at that level is wide, but the +90.1% gain is several times larger.
- The peak RSS `run.sh` reads for `entry-cores` (1.9 MiB) is the supervisor's alone, so it is left out of these tables. The one process peaked at 91 MiB on `/plaintext` and 3.1 MiB on `/json`.
- The TechEmpower image runs one worker per core of the machine (`nish.dockerfile`). TFB's own servers have more cores than this VM, so these figures say only that the second core pays off here.

## TechEmpower: the async entry against the explicit one (WP49 stage 6)

[WP49](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/docs/wp49-async-server.md#6-tfb-what-the-top-takes)'s gate: the TechEmpower entry written in the `async` style must stay inside the noise band of the same entry written in the explicit style, on `plaintext`, `json` and `db`. Measured by `bash bench/techempower/run.sh`, which times `async-main.ts` beside `main.ts` and its byte-identical copy, and `async-db-main.ts` beside `db-main.ts` and its byte-identical copy on all five database tests, and copied here from `build/tfb/results.md`. The band is the gap between each pair of identical builds' medians, from the same run. Every contestant pair is started with the same arguments: one process, the same slots, one worker and 4 Postgres connections for the database entries.

**This is a relative A/B on one machine, not a TechEmpower rank.** wrk, Postgres and the server share one 4-core VM, so the figures record their contention as well as the server; [D6](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/docs/wp49-async-server.md#9-decisions) stands, and nothing here is a public number. The cells are req/s, median (minimum) of 3 interleaved rounds of 10 s, each contestant started afresh and warmed for 1 s. Peak RSS is the server's `VmHWM` from `/proc`, the largest over every round and concurrency of the test (for `/plaintext` that is 16384 connections' buffers). A `*` is a cell with non-2xx answers or socket errors in a round, listed after the tables. wrk counts a non-2xx answer in its rate, so a `*` figure may be inflated by fast failures; the verdict line marks it `*` and never calls it inside. The database is loaded once and not reset between contestants or rounds, so `/updates`, which rewrites `world` in place, leaves later measurements more dead tuples than earlier ones; the rotated order spreads that across the contestants, and the explicit entry, whose `/updates` rate is more than twice the async entry's, adds more of them. That count is inferred from the req/s figures; the updates were not counted.

| | |
| --- | --- |
| Date | 2026-11-10 |
| nish | `build/nish` at `da6472d` (nish 0.37.0 sources), `--link --profile speed` |
| CPU | Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.10GHz, 4 logical cores, 15 GiB |
| OS | linux 6.18.44 (x64) |
| Postgres | 16, a throwaway cluster in `build/tfb/pg`, `fsync=off`, TFB's `world` (10,000 rows) and `fortune` tables |
| Client | wrk 4.1.0, TFB's request headers, `--timeout 8`; `/plaintext` through `bench/techempower/pipeline.lua` 16 deep |
| Pinning | plaintext and json: server on CPU 0, wrk `-t 3` on CPUs 1-3. Database: server on CPU 0, Postgres on CPU 1, wrk `-t 2` on CPUs 2-3 |
| Command | `npm run build && PG_BIN=/usr/lib/postgresql/16/bin bash bench/techempower/run.sh` |

This is the first measurement, kept as the before; the [re-measurement after the serve and pool fixes](#techempower-the-async-entry-re-measured-after-the-serve-and-pool-fixes-wp49-stage-6) follows it.

**Outcome: the gate is not met.** The async entry is outside the band at every `plaintext` concurrency (about 87–90% slower), at five of six `json` concurrencies (1.5–12.4% slower, inside only at 256), and on every database test except `cached-queries` at 256 connections (42–91% slower).

Why. The figures below come from `strace -c`, which slows both servers about 9 to 18 times. So read them as counts per request, not as throughput. Each server was started on CPU 0 and warmed for 2 s by the same wrk command. Then `strace -c -f -p <pid>` was attached for one 3 s wrk run, on 2026-11-10 at `13292203`:

```bash
taskset -c 0 build/tfb/<entry|async> <port> &
# warm: the test's wrk command below, with -d 2s
strace -c -f -p <pid> &   # attached for the one measured run, stopped with SIGINT after it
taskset -c 1-3 wrk -t 3 -c 256 -d 3s -H "Host: tfb-server" http://127.0.0.1:<port>/plaintext -s bench/techempower/pipeline.lua -- 16
taskset -c 1-3 wrk -t 3 -c 64 -d 3s -H "Host: tfb-server" http://127.0.0.1:<port>/json
```

`async`, `/json`, 64 connections (24894 requests in 3.00s, 3.54MB read):

```text
% time     seconds  usecs/call     calls    errors syscall
------ ----------- ----------- --------- --------- ----------------
 63.61    0.491892          19     25302           sendto
 31.93    0.246869           9     25750       396 recvfrom
  1.65    0.012736          16       796           epoll_wait
  1.44    0.011107          12       859           epoll_ctl
  0.48    0.003748           9       399           close
  0.48    0.003720           9       397           fcntl

  0.07    0.000563           8        64           getpeername
  0.07    0.000548           8        64           setsockopt
------ ----------- ----------- --------- --------- ----------------
100.00    0.773261          14     53824       397 total
```

</details>

- **`plaintext`.**
  - The explicit entry answers inside `Http1Server`'s event loop and sends the 16 pipelined answers of one read in one `sendto`: 25,379 for 402,880 requests.
  - `serve` takes a connection's next request only once the one before it is answered (`std/net/serve.ts`, "at most one request per connection is in flight"), and `HttpHandler` flushes each answer as it ends (`end` → `drive` → `flush`). So a 16-deep burst becomes 16 task runs, each with its own `sendto`: 44,857 for 43,646 requests.
  - That is about one `sendto` per request against one per 16. The per-turn calls below are a few hundred in all at this load, so they are not the main share here. How much of the rest is the send and how much the per-request task run was not separated.
- **`json`** is not pipelined, so each entry makes one `sendto` per answer (26,759 for 26,741 requests; 25,302 for 24,894), and the send count does not explain its gap.
  - What the async entry adds is a fixed cost per turn of the executor's loop. `nish_async_wait_fd` duplicates the descriptor (`fcntl(F_DUPFD_CLOEXEC)`) and adds it to epoll, `nish_async_fire` removes and closes it, and `serve` writes a wake byte and drains it (the extra `sendto` and the failing `recvfrom`).
  - Here that is about 400 turns: 397 `fcntl`, 399 `close`, 859 `epoll_ctl`. Those are about 2,600 calls on 54,000, or 0.1 a request.
  - So the syscall count does not account for 7–12% either. The rest is CPU per request in user space (the task, its frame, the handler's path), which this run did not profile. The cause of `json`'s gap is open.
- **`db`, `queries`, `updates`, `fortunes`:** `PgAsyncPool` sends one query per connection at a time, where the explicit pool pipelines every request's queries down its 4 connections. A task that finds every connection claimed sleeps 1 ms doubling to 16 ms rather than being woken by a release. The non-2xx answers at 64 and 256 connections are claims that waited out `claimWaitMs`. That is read from the code, not from a trace.
- **`cached-queries`** reads Postgres only to fill the cache, so it is the one database test near the band. The async database entry's peak RSS is lower (12–21 MiB against 65–66 MiB): the explicit entry reserves its carrier's query room up front.

## TechEmpower: the async entry re-measured after the serve and pool fixes (WP49 stage 6)

The same gate as [the first measurement](#techempower-the-async-entry-against-the-explicit-one-wp49-stage-6), which stays above as the before, run again on `main` once `serve` answered a pipelined burst in one write (#1378) and the async Postgres pool pipelined queries across tasks (#1379). The method, the pinning, the `*` rule and the caveats are the first measurement's: one 4-core VM shared by wrk, Postgres and the server, so a relative A/B and not a TechEmpower rank ([D6](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/docs/wp49-async-server.md#9-decisions)). The cells are req/s, median (minimum) of 3 interleaved rounds of 10 s, and the band is the gap between each pair of identical builds' medians in this run. `run.sh`'s inside rule is one-sided: a delta `d` is inside when `d >= −band`, so an async median above the entry's counts as inside whatever the band. That is how `/updates` is inside at 256 connections (+4.8% against a 2.5% band) and `/queries` at 16 (+3.0% against 3.0%): not slower by more than the noise, rather than level within it. One caveat of the first measurement no longer holds: the explicit entry no longer makes more `/updates` than the async one (the async entry is level or above it), so it no longer adds more of `world`'s dead tuples. Peak RSS is the server's `VmHWM`, the largest over every round and concurrency of the test.

| | |
| --- | --- |
| Date | 2026-11-11 |
| nish | `build/nish` at `e523e14` (nish 0.38.0 sources), `--link --profile speed` |
| CPU | Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.10GHz, 4 logical cores, 15 GiB |
| OS | linux 6.18.44 (x64) |
| Postgres | 16, a throwaway cluster in `build/tfb/pg`, `fsync=off`, TFB's `world` (10,000 rows) and `fortune` tables |
| Client | wrk 4.1.0, TFB's request headers, `--timeout 8`; `/plaintext` through `bench/techempower/pipeline.lua` 16 deep |
| Pinning | plaintext and json: server on CPU 0, wrk `-t 3` on CPUs 1-3. Database: server on CPU 0, Postgres on CPU 1, wrk `-t 2` on CPUs 2-3 |
| Command | `npm run build && PG_BIN=/usr/lib/postgresql/16/bin bash bench/techempower/run.sh` |

**Outcome: the gate is still not met.** The fixes moved the async entry a long way, but it is outside the band on all three of the gate's tests:

- **`plaintext`**: 32–38% below the explicit entry at 256, 1,024 and 4,096 connections (it was 87–90%), and 70.9% below at 16,384, a `*` cell.
- **`json`**: 2.5–17.1% below at every concurrency, against bands of 0.5–2.5%. The first run's one `inside` cell, at 256 connections, is now outside at −6.3% against a 0.5% band.
- **`db`**: 74.2% and 79.4% below at 64 and 256 connections (it was 90.0% and 90.5%). The 16-connection cell cannot be judged: its band is not a usable one (below), so the verdict rests on 64 and 256.

Of the database tests outside the gate, `/updates` is inside at all three concurrencies, `/queries` and `/cached-queries` are inside at 16 and 16–64 connections, and `/fortunes` is outside everywhere.

The `/db` band at 16 connections is not a usable one. Both explicit builds came out in one of two modes, about 23–30k or 72–75k req/s, from round to round (`db-entry` 28,234, 72,518 and 75,337; `db-entry-copy` 74,689, 30,208 and 22,957), so the band measures which mode each median landed in. The async entry gave 27.7–28.5k in every round, the low mode, so against a single mode it could be level or 62% below. `run.sh` prints outside; this record does not judge the cell, and the `db` verdict rests on 64 and 256 connections. The cause of the two modes was not looked into. `/cached-queries` at 16 connections has a 9.3% band from the same kind of spread, so its `inside` is a weak one.

No database cell has a `*` this time; the first run had five, all the async entry's, from ten rounds with non-2xx answers while it waited for a connection.

**Memory.** The async entry's peak RSS is above the explicit entry's on `plaintext` (95.8 against 91.2 MiB, at 16,384 connections) and on `json` (7.6 against 3.1 MiB). On the database tests it is below (18.3–29.5 against 65.3–66.1 MiB), because the explicit entry reserves its carrier's query room up front. Against the first run, the async entry's peak rose on every test (`plaintext` 91.5 → 95.8, `json` 3.3 → 7.6, database 12.1–20.8 → 18.3–29.5 MiB).

**Why, as far as this run goes.** The `strace -c` counts were taken the same way as the first run's, on 2026-11-11 at `e523e14`. They slow both servers several times, so read them as calls per request, not throughput:

```bash
taskset -c 0 build/tfb/<entry|async> <port> &
# warm: the test's wrk command below, with -d 2s
strace -c -f -p <pid> &   # attached for the one measured run, stopped with SIGINT after it
taskset -c 1-3 wrk -t 3 -c 256 -d 3s -H "Host: tfb-server" http://127.0.0.1:<port>/plaintext -s bench/techempower/pipeline.lua -- 16
taskset -c 1-3 wrk -t 3 -c 64 -d 3s -H "Host: tfb-server" http://127.0.0.1:<port>/json
```

- **`plaintext`.** #1378 removed the send gap: the async entry now makes one `sendto` per pipelined burst, as the explicit loop does (18,943 for 301,616 requests, against 20,440 for 326,288). Its extra calls per executor turn (297 `fcntl`, 299 `close`, 851 `epoll_ctl`) are about 0.005 a request. So system calls no longer account for the remaining 32–38%. What is left is CPU per request in user space: the async entry still runs one task turn per request where the explicit loop answers a burst inside one read. That was not profiled. At 16,384 connections the async entry also had wrk read errors in every round, which the explicit entry had in one copy's round; their cause was not looked into.
- **`json`** is unchanged in kind from the first run: one `sendto` per answer on both sides (23,286 for 23,239; 21,612 for 21,584), and about 345 executor turns' `fcntl`/`close`/`epoll_ctl`, about 0.07 calls a request. The 2.5–17.1% is CPU per request in user space, and it was not profiled. #1378's `json` figures were already mixed and recorded open; the cause is still open.
- **`db`, `queries`, `fortunes`.** The async entry now gains less, or loses, as connections rise, where the explicit entry gains: `/db` 27.8k → 21.6k from 16 to 256 connections against 72.5k → 105.0k; `/queries` 24.8k → 19.0k against 24.1k → 36.0k; `/fortunes` 32.1k → 17.2k against 55.9k → 65.8k. With #1379 a connection takes at most 8 seated statements (its default depth), each sent with its own Sync, and a task queues for a seat when all 4 connections are full; the explicit carrier pipelines every in-flight request's query. Whether the depth, the per-statement Sync or the task turns limit it was not traced, so the cause is open. #1379 already recorded `/fortunes` at 64 and more connections as slower than the previous async pool, cause unmeasured; this run agrees (22.1k and 17.2k against the first run's 25.0k and 22.9k).
- **`updates`** is inside the band at every concurrency. Its 20 updates per request are sent as one statement by both entries, which suggests Postgres's write path, not the pool, sets the rate there; that is inferred from the figures, not traced.


## TechEmpower: where the async entry's time goes, a profile (WP49 stage 6)

[The re-measurement](#techempower-the-async-entry-re-measured-after-the-serve-and-pool-fixes-wp49-stage-6) left `plaintext`, `json` and `db` outside the gate, and their system calls do not explain it. This profile asks where each request's time goes in user space, on the async entry against the explicit one, so that the next fixes have a target. It measures; it fixes nothing. `bash bench/techempower/profile.sh` reproduces it, and `--help` says the method.

| | |
| --- | --- |
| Date | 2026-11-11 |
| Sources | `main` at `0e338d83`. Each table's header names the branch commit the script ran at, which adds only `profile.sh`. `/plaintext` ran at `b26ed0b4` and `/json` and `/db` at `ddd1240c`. Between the two, the script changed only the `/db` cluster's setup, which also loads `fortune` because the entries prepare its statement at start. The method did not change. |
| Build | `build/nish <entry> --link --profile speed -g`. The `-g` keeps the symbols. The `-g` build's text is within 1.2% of the build without it, by `size`: 72,687 bytes for the explicit plaintext entry against 73,535 without `-g` (1.2%), and 137,260 for the async one against 137,996 (0.5%) |
| CPU | Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.10GHz, 4 logical cores (a cloud VM, no hardware performance counters) |
| Load | `/plaintext` at 256 connections, 16 pipelined (`pipeline.lua`); `/json` at 64; `/db` at 64 against `run.sh`'s throwaway Postgres (port 15433), one worker and 4 Postgres connections on both sides |
| Pinning | server on CPU 0; wrk on CPUs 1–3, or 2–3 with Postgres on CPU 1 for `/db` |

Each figure comes from one tool, run in its own phase so that no tool's overhead lands in another's figure. The phases alternate between the two entries.

- **Instructions per request (Ir):** callgrind (valgrind 3.22). The server starts with `--instr-atstart=no`; `callgrind_control -i on` and `-i off` bracket a 10 s wrk window, and Ir per request is the window's program total divided by wrk's responses. Requests in flight at the window's edges are counted on the wrong side, so a figure can be off by at most the in-flight requests over the responses: 4,096 (256 × 16) on `/plaintext`, about 3.5% of the async window; 64 on `/json` and `/db`, under 0.4%. `callgrind_annotate --auto=no` lists the frames, and the script sums a function's lines across the files it was inlined from.
- **Top user-space frames:** `perf record -e cpu-clock:u -F 2999 -g` (perf 6.8.12) over a 5 s wrk window. `cpu-clock:u` is a software event, since the VM exposes no hardware counters. The share is of user-space samples only.
- **System calls per request:** `strace -c -f -p` over a 5 s wrk window.
- **req/s and CPU per request:** wrk and the server's `utime + stime` in `/proc/<pid>/stat`, over the perf window. Both are under perf, so they are not the gate's figures.

| test | Ir per request, explicit → async | system calls per request | CPU µs per request | req/s under perf |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `/plaintext` (256, 16 pipelined) | 5,419 → 11,662 (+6,243, ×2.15) | 0.132 → 0.137 | 0.93 → 1.58 | 1,056k → 629k |
| `/json` (64) | 6,614 → 13,153 (+6,539, ×1.99) | 2.037 → 2.116 | 7.37 → 8.13 | 135k → 122k |
| `/db` (64) | 11,017 → 37,229 (+26,212, ×3.38) | 2.100 → 24.008 | 9.96 → 39.70 | 81.3k → 24.5k |

All six perf runs were clean (no wrk socket errors, no non-2xx answers). The codec's frames (`http1Next`, `http1TakeLine`, `http1WriteResponseHead`) cost the same on both sides of every test. The difference is in the layer the async entry puts between the codec and the handler, and on `/db` in the pool. The causes below are ranked by the Ir per request each accounts for, from the frames' self Ir (callgrind), with the source line where the count is a line's. The one exception is `/db`'s cause 2, which is kernel time with no Ir figure: it is placed by its system calls (strace) and CPU per request (`/proc`). [WP49 §6](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/docs/wp49-async-server.md#6-tfb-what-the-top-takes) carries the same ranking as the plan for the fixes.

**`/plaintext` and `/json`** (fix-serve). The same functions make the gap on both. They are ranked by the two tests' Ir summed, and each function's self Ir per request is netted against the explicit side's (plaintext first, then json):

1. **`serveTask` copies each request in and each answer out, byte by byte** (`std/net/serve.ts`), with `HttpRequest.constructor`: 3,158 and 3,428. Of plaintext's: the head copy (`head[i] = src.head[i]` and its loop) about 1,880, the scan for the head's end about 220, the spans about 225, and the new arrays and their zeroing about 170. Then the answer is copied back into the slot (`out[i] = res.body[i]`), about 190, and its content type a character at a time (`type[i] = toU8(res.type.charCodeAt(i))`), about 160.
2. **The `HttpHandler` layer** (`std/net/handler.ts`): `drive`, `start`, `write`, `end`, `enqueue`, `settle` and `flush`, and the `Http1Server`/`Http1Connection` calls they make, 1,313 and 1,596.
3. **`serveOn`'s scans** (`std/net/serve.ts`): 690 and 896, mostly the pass over every slot after each turn (`serveFreeSlot`'s scan about 160, then the `busy`/`done` checks) and `serveType`'s byte compare of the content-type cache, about 70.
4. **The task per request and its memory**: `nish_async_spawn`, `nish_async_done`, `malloc`/`free`/`_int_free` and `nish_arena_release`, 707 and 704.
5. **`Router.match` and `Router.walk`** (`std/net/router.ts`), which the explicit entry does not run: 789 and 562. `routerSameBytes` compares the path a byte at a time.

Against these, the explicit loop's own dispatch (`TfbServer.resume` and `nish_main`, 655 and 919) has no async counterpart. Net of it, the five account for about 6,000 of plaintext's 6,243 and 6,270 of json's 6,539. On `/plaintext` the user-space path is most of a request, so the async entry's CPU per request is 1.7 times the explicit one's. On `/json` the kernel's share of a request (one receive and one send each) is large, so twice the instructions show as 10% more CPU, within the 2.5–17.1% of json's gap in the gate.

**`/db`** (fix-pool; causes 3 and 4 are `serve`'s and go with fix-serve). Causes 1, 3, 4 and 5 are ranked by Ir. Cause 2 is ranked by system calls and CPU time, because its cost is in the kernel:

1. **`pgAsyncRead` zero-fills a fresh 8 KiB buffer per answer** (`std/net/postgres-async.ts`, `new Array<u8>(… PG_ASYNC_INPUT_SIZE)`): `__memset_avx2_unaligned_erms` is 8,360 Ir a request, 22% of the async total, 8,211 of it called from `pgAsyncAnswer`. The answer is then copied twice, a byte at a time: into `carried` in `pgAsyncAnswer` and into that buffer in `pgAsyncRead`, about 1,400. About 9,800 in all.
2. **Two waits per statement, and the system calls they cost.** Each request waits about 2.7 times in `nish_async_wait_fd`: once in `pgAsyncPark` on the pool's wake socket pair for its turn (1.04 a request), once in `pgAsyncTurn` on the Postgres socket (0.99), and `serveOn`'s own wait (0.71). The Postgres socket is also waited on, through `readable(fd)`, in `pgAsyncRead` when a read answers `EAGAIN`. That is where most of the 22 extra system calls a request go: 6.0 `epoll_ctl`, 3.0 `fcntl`, 3.0 `close`, 2.0 `epoll_wait`, 3.0 more `sendto` and 4.9 more `recvfrom`, 1.9 of which answer `EAGAIN`, against the explicit entry's one `sendto` and one `recvfrom`. The Ir is small; the cost is kernel time, most of the 39.7 µs against 9.96. What one wait costs was settled in #1374. What is open here is the number of waits the pool takes per statement.
3. **`serveOn` scans every slot on each pass** (`std/net/serve.ts`), for fix-serve: 5,690 self here against 690 on `/plaintext`, because a request's task stays in flight across its Postgres round trip, so more slots are busy. The `busy && done` check costs about 980, the `busy && hungUp` check about 870, and the loop 640. The `hungUp` call into `HttpHandler.hungUp` and `Http1Server.holds` costs about 1,600. Together, about 4,100.
4. **`serveTask`'s copies**, as on `/plaintext`: 3,988 self.
5. **The seat bookkeeping**: `pgAsyncSkippable` (4 calls a request, each a loop over `PG_ASYNC_MAX_DEPTH`), `pgAsyncSeat`, `pgAsyncSend`, `pgAsyncReady`, `pgAsyncTurn`, `pgAsyncScan`, `pgAsyncSignal` and `pgAsyncPark`, about 3,600 self, against about 1,300 for the explicit carrier's `PgCarrier.next`, `PgCarrier.dispatch` and `TfbDb.turn`. So about 2,300 net.

Net of the explicit carrier's 1,300, the four with an Ir figure (1, 3, 4 and 5) account for about 20,200 of the 26,212.

## TechEmpower: the async entry re-measured after the profile-led fixes (WP49 stage 6)

The same gate as [the first measurement](#techempower-the-async-entry-against-the-explicit-one-wp49-stage-6) and [the 2026-11-11 re-measurement](#techempower-the-async-entry-re-measured-after-the-serve-and-pool-fixes-wp49-stage-6), which stay above as the befores, run again once [the profile](#techempower-where-the-async-entrys-time-goes-a-profile-wp49-stage-6) (#1410) had ranked where the async entry's time goes and three PRs had acted on it: #1421 cut `serve`'s per-request copies and slot scans, #1419 cut the async Postgres pool's buffer fill and waits and batched the async entry's concurrent `/db` ids into one statement, and #1459 trimmed the router's walk and `serveTask`'s copies. The method, the pinning, the `*` rule and the caveats are the first measurement's: one 4-core VM shared by wrk, Postgres and the server, so a relative A/B and not a TechEmpower rank ([D6](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/docs/wp49-async-server.md#9-decisions)). The cells are req/s, median (minimum) of 3 interleaved rounds of 10 s, and the band is the gap between each pair of identical builds' medians in this run. `run.sh`'s inside rule is one-sided: a delta `d` is inside when `d >= −band`. Peak RSS is the server's `VmHWM`, the largest over every round and concurrency of the test.

The tree measured is #1459's head `f9ffe352` merged locally with `main` at `747126db`, which #1459 had not yet taken in (#1444, #1448, #1452). The merge's only conflict is `tests/nish-cmp.js`, which no benchmark binary reads. #1448 makes the explicit entry fork one worker a CPU by default, but the worker count is `availableParallelism()`, which reads the affinity mask, and `run.sh` starts every server under `taskset -c 0`: the explicit entry still runs as one process, as in the earlier runs.

| | |
| --- | --- |
| Date | 2026-11-12 |
| nish | `build/nish` built from #1459's head `f9ffe352` merged locally with `main` at `747126db`, a merge that has no published SHA (nish 0.38.0 sources), `--link --profile speed` |
| CPU | Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.10GHz, 4 logical cores, 15 GiB |
| OS | linux 6.18.44 (x64) |
| Postgres | 16, a throwaway cluster in `build/tfb/pg`, `fsync=off`, TFB's `world` (10,000 rows) and `fortune` tables |
| Client | wrk 4.1.0, TFB's request headers, `--timeout 8`; `/plaintext` through `bench/techempower/pipeline.lua` 16 deep |
| Pinning | plaintext and json: server on CPU 0, wrk `-t 3` on CPUs 1-3. Database: server on CPU 0, Postgres on CPU 1, wrk `-t 2` on CPUs 2-3 |
| Command | `npm run build && PG_BIN=/usr/lib/postgresql/16/bin bash bench/techempower/run.sh` |

**Outcome: the gate is still not met.** Six of the eleven judged cells are outside:

- **`plaintext`**: outside at all three judged concurrencies, 27.7%, 15.0% and 26.9% below the explicit entry at 256, 1,024 and 4,096 connections (they were 32–38% below). At 16,384, recorded and not judged, it is 68.0% below, a `*` cell again.
- **`json`**: inside at 16, 32, 64 and 256 connections and outside at 128 (−4.9% against a 0.5% band) and 512 (−13.0% against 5.7%). The 256 cell is inside on a 9.5% band, so it is a weak inside.
- **`db`**: outside at 64 connections (−5.7% against a 2.2% band) and inside at 256 (+7.3%). It was 74.2% and 79.4% below. The 16-connection cell is bimodal again (below) and recorded, not judged.

Of the database tests outside the gate, `/updates` and `/cached-queries` are inside at all three concurrencies, `/queries` at 16 and 64 connections, and `/fortunes` nowhere.

The `/db` band at 16 connections is bimodal as it was on 2026-11-11: each explicit build landed at about 24–25k or 72–75k req/s from round to round (`db-entry` 24,250, 72,613 and 75,228; `db-entry-copy` 75,169, 25,484 and 24,521), so the band measures which mode each median fell in. The async entry gave 47.3–57.0k. `run.sh` prints inside; this record does not judge the cell, and the `db` verdict rests on 64 and 256 connections. The cause of the two modes is still not looked into. The `/plaintext` band at 256 connections is wide for one round: `entry-copy`'s first round gave 1,067,409 against 1,428,775 and 1,240,902 in its others. The async entry, at 1,005,222–1,030,827, is below every explicit round of both builds, so the cell is outside however that round is read.

The async entry now beats the explicit one on `/queries` at 16 and 64 connections, on `/updates` and on `/cached-queries`. The gain is most likely the pool's changes in #1419; that is inferred, not traced.

No database cell has a `*`, and neither explicit build has one anywhere. The async entry's read errors at 16,384 connections, in every round as on 2026-11-11, are still not looked into.

**Memory.** On `plaintext` and `json` the async entry's peak RSS is where it was on 2026-11-11 (95.8 MiB against the explicit entry's 91.4, and 7.6 against 3.3). On the database tests it stays below the explicit entry's (18.7–33.1 against 65.4–66.2 MiB), but `/db`'s rose from 22.6 to 33.1 MiB, which follows #1419's batching of concurrent ids into one statement; that is inferred from the timing, not traced.

**What remains, as far as this run and the profiles go.** No new profile was taken for this run; #1459's callgrind tables, on its head, are the latest (its PR body has them). Per request, the async entry still runs about 3,100 (`plaintext`) and 3,300 (`json`) more instructions than the explicit one, about 8,500 against 5,400 and 9,900 against 6,600. About 750 of it is the task's memory in `runtime/runtime-async.c` (`malloc`/`free` of the frame and region, `nish_async_spawn`, `nish_async_done`, `nish_arena_release`), not the per-wait system calls #1374 ruled out; about 2,100 is the layered hand-off from `serveOn` through `HttpHandler` to `serveTask` and the router's walk; about 190 (`plaintext`) and 450 (`json`) is shared `std/net/http1-server.ts` and JSON-writer code. The database tests' remaining gap was not profiled after #1419.

## TechEmpower: the gate's baseline checked and re-measured (WP49 stage 6)

The same gate as [the re-measurement after the profile-led fixes](#techempower-the-async-entry-re-measured-after-the-profile-led-fixes-wp49-stage-6), run on `main` to answer one question and to give the next fixes their before: since #1448 `bench/techempower/main.ts` forks one worker a core when it is given no worker count, `run.sh` gives `entry` and `entry-copy` none, and `async-main.ts` is always one process. Is the gate still one worker against one worker?

**It is, and it was in the earlier runs.** The default count is `availableParallelism()`, which reads the affinity mask, and `run.sh` starts every server under `taskset -c 0`. Each default contestant was started as `run.sh` starts it and read from `/proc` while `wrk` (256 connections on `/json`, on CPUs 1-3) loaded it: the processes in its tree, each one's `Cpus_allowed_list` and threads from `/proc/<pid>/status`, and its CPU ticks (`utime + stime` of `/proc/<pid>/stat`) 1.5 s into the load.

| started as | processes | `Cpus_allowed_list` | threads | CPU ticks at 1.5 s |
| --- | ---: | --- | ---: | ---: |
| `taskset -c 0 build/tfb/entry <port>` (`run.sh`'s `entry`) | 1 | 0 | 1 | 147 |
| `taskset -c 0 build/tfb/entry-copy <port>` (`run.sh`'s `entry-copy`) | 1 | 0 | 1 | 150 |
| `taskset -c 0 build/tfb/async <port>` (`run.sh`'s `async`) | 1 | 0 | 1 | 151 |
| `taskset -c 0 build/tfb/entry <port> 1 127.0.0.1 16384 1` (the explicit count 1) | 1 | 0 | 1 | 150 |
| `build/tfb/entry <port>`, unpinned | 5: a supervisor and 4 workers | supervisor 0-3; workers 0, 1, 2 and 3 | 1 each | 0; 0, 67, 68 and 66 |
| `taskset -c 0-1 build/tfb/entry <port>` | 3: a supervisor and 2 workers | supervisor 0-1; workers 0 and 1 | 1 each | 0; 143 and 110 |
| `NISH_WORKERS=4 taskset -c 0 build/tfb/entry <port>` | none: exit 1, `nish/net/prefork: pin with 4 workers on 1 CPUs` | | | |

So under `run.sh`'s pinning the explicit entry serves in the process it was started in, with no supervisor and no worker, exactly as it does given the count 1, and the three contestants each have one thread on CPU 0. The last three rows are what the gate does not run: without the pin the entry is one worker a core, and with `NISH_WORKERS` above 1 exported it refuses to start, so `run.sh` exits 1 (`entry did not start`) and cannot compare unlike things silently. The database wrappers pass the count 1, and `profile.sh` starts its servers under the same `taskset -c 0`.

**What changed in `run.sh`: its comment only.** The header said "workers (one)" without saying what makes it one; it now says the count is `main.ts`'s default under the pin, and what `NISH_WORKERS` does. The contestants' commands are the ones the earlier runs used, so the earlier sections' figures were one worker against one worker too.

| Date | 2026-11-12 |

| CPU | Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.10GHz, 4 logical cores, 15 GiB |
| OS | linux 6.18.44 (x64) |

| Commands | `bash bench/techempower/run.sh --only plaintext`, `bash bench/techempower/run.sh --only json --duration 8`, `PG_BIN=/usr/lib/postgresql/16/bin bash bench/techempower/run.sh --only db --duration 2`, each alone in the foreground; `bash bench/techempower/profile.sh --only plaintext` and `--only json` |

The method, the `*` rule and the caveats are [the first measurement](#techempower-the-async-entry-against-the-explicit-one-wp49-stage-6)'s: one 4-core VM shared by wrk, Postgres and the server, a relative A/B and not a TechEmpower rank. The cells are req/s, median (minimum) of 3 interleaved rounds, and a delta `d` is inside when `d >= −band`. **The rounds are shorter than the earlier runs' on two tests**, because one call of this session's shell ends after ten minutes and each test ran as one call: `/plaintext` has the default 10 s, `/json` 8 s, and the five database tests 2 s. A 2 s round is a weak figure; the database rows below are a baseline for a re-run at the same `--duration 2`, not a replacement for the 10 s figures of the section above. The http-twins were not built, so the tables have no twin rows.

### The baseline profile: instructions and system calls per request

`profile.sh` on the same tree, its defaults (5 s perf and strace phases, a 10 s callgrind window), one process on CPU 0 for each side as above. Instructions per request (callgrind's Ir over the responses wrk counted in the window) hold across machines and noise, so they are the figure a fix is checked against.

| | explicit | async | async − explicit |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| `/plaintext`, 256 connections, 16 pipelined: Ir per request | 4,814 | 7,848 | 3,034 |
| `/plaintext`: system calls per request (`strace -c -f`) | 0.129 | 0.134 | 0.005 |
| `/plaintext`: req/s under perf | 1,326,148 | 982,824 | |
| `/plaintext`: server CPU µs per request, user + system | 0.74 | 1.01 | |
| `/json`, 64 connections: Ir per request | 5,962 | 9,276 | 3,314 |
| `/json`: system calls per request (`strace -c -f`) | 2.026 | 2.109 | 0.083 |
| `/json`: req/s under perf | 136,267 | 136,230 | |
| `/json`: server CPU µs per request, user + system | 7.30 | 7.23 | |

The responses in the callgrind windows were 343,648 and 225,104 (`plaintext`, explicit and async) and 155,028 and 160,147 (`json`); no wrk run of either profile had an error. The system calls are almost all `recvfrom` and `sendto`, one of each a request on `/json` and one of each for about sixteen on `/plaintext`. What the async entry adds over the 5 s strace window is its per-wait calls: on `/plaintext` 1,375 `epoll_ctl`, 561 `close` and 559 `fcntl` against the explicit entry's 256 `epoll_ctl`, over 35,711 `sendto`; on `/json` 1,301, 620 and 618 against 64, over 38,848.

The explicit entry's Ir is lower than the 5,411 (`plaintext`) and 6,588 (`json`) of #1459's tables, and the async entry's lower than its 8,507 and 9,887, with the difference between the two entries about the same (3,034 and 3,314 against about 3,100 and 3,300). What moved both was not measured; #1460's request-head parse landed on `main` after that head, and `http1Next` is in both entries. The top frames by self Ir per request:

**Until stage 6's re-measurement after task recycling ([below](#techempower-the-gate-after-task-recycling-wp49-stage-6)), the gate was read against this section**: a fix's before is these tables, re-run with the same commands and durations, and its Ir per request is set against the 4,814 and 7,848 (`plaintext`) and 5,962 and 9,276 (`json`) above.

## TechEmpower: the gate after task recycling (WP49 stage 6)

This re-runs the gate on `main` after two changes.
- **Task recycling (#1493):** a finished task's node keeps its region for the node's next task, so a request's task costs no `malloc` or `free`.
- **Response head (#1457):** a repeated HTTP/1 response head is built once per `Date` tick.

The changes are library and runtime changes that serve every `nish:async` and `serve` program; nothing in `bench/techempower/` moved. Two other candidates were measured and not landed, because what a user would gain does not pay for them:
- **Reading the request in place:** `serveTask` would hand `handle` the connection slot's buffers in place of a copy. The language's task-memory rule refuses this: `handle` may grow what it is given. The copy it would save costs about 330 Ir per request.
- **A fixed-path table in `Router`:** callgrind (valgrind 3.22.0), Ir per match.
  - On `examples/http-app`'s routes: 506 → 491 for `/health`, unchanged for `/notes/17`.
  - On a 44-route router: 591 → 491 and 909 → 765 for fixed paths, unchanged for paths with parameters, and 527 → 570 for a 404.

| nish | `build/nish` built from `main` at `5c0cd6b2` (nish 0.38.0 sources), `--link --profile speed` |
| CPU | Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.10GHz, 4 logical cores, 15 GiB |
| OS | linux 6.18.44 (x64) |
| Postgres | 16, a throwaway cluster in `build/tfb/pg`, as [the baseline](#techempower-the-gates-baseline-checked-and-re-measured-wp49-stage-6) |
| Client | wrk 4.1.0, TFB's request headers, `--timeout 8`; `/plaintext` through `bench/techempower/pipeline.lua` 16 deep |
| Pinning | plaintext and json: server on CPU 0, wrk `-t 3` on CPUs 1-3. Database: server on CPU 0, Postgres on CPU 1, wrk `-t 2` on CPUs 2-3 |
| Commands | `bash bench/techempower/run.sh --only plaintext`, then `--only json`, then `--only db`, one after another with nothing else running; `run.sh` unchanged |

Every test ran the default 3 rounds of 10 s. So `/json` and the database tests are at the durations of [the re-measurement after the profile-led fixes](#techempower-the-async-entry-re-measured-after-the-profile-led-fixes-wp49-stage-6), not the baseline's 8 s and 2 s. The method, the band and the `*` rule are unchanged. The http-twins were not built.

**Outcome: the gate is not met.** Four of the six cells #1468 and #1402 judge are outside the band:

The judged set is the six cells #1468 lists, narrower than the earlier sections' eleven: the cells the explicit entry's issue tracks, not every cell `run.sh` prints. `run.sh` also prints `/json` at 16, 64 and 256 as outside (−8.4%, −3.2%, −10.6%, in the tables below); they are recorded and not judged, as are `/plaintext` at 16,384, `/db` at 16 and 256, and the other database tests.

| test | connections | band | async vs entry | verdict | the baseline |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- | ---: |
| `/plaintext` | 256 | 1.3% | −34.5% | outside | −27.6% |
| `/plaintext` | 1024 | 4.4% | −24.1% | outside | −21.7% |
| `/plaintext` | 4096 | 2.9% | −25.7% | outside | −31.3% |
| `/json` | 128 | 10.0% | +7.3% | inside | −11.6% |
| `/json` | 512 | 3.7% | −17.0% | outside | −13.1% |
| `/db` | 64 | 7.9% | +4.9% | inside | −9.1% |

Reading the rounds:

- **`/plaintext`:** every async round is below every explicit round, at all three connection counts. For example, at 256 connections the async entry gave 1,122,886 to 1,131,320 req/s, and `entry` and `entry-copy` gave 1,626,321 to 1,730,805. The cells are outside however the band is read. #1457 builds the head once per tick for both entries, so it does not narrow the gap. At 4,096 connections the delta moved from −31.3% to −25.7%, and at 256 it moved from −27.6% to −34.5%, so this run does not show a trend.
- **`/json` at 128:** the async entry gave 182,829 to 185,514 req/s, inside the explicit rounds' 170,948 to 192,047. The cell is inside on its rounds as well as on its median.
- **`/json` at 512:** `entry` is bimodal (166,056, 215,934 and 217,296 req/s). The async entry's best round, 181,014, is below every `entry-copy` round (191,532 to 218,709). The cell is outside.
- **`/db` at 64:** this cell also has one low explicit round, `db-entry`'s first, at 22,253 against 108,106 and 113,153. That widens the band to 7.9%. The async entry's rounds, 112,066 to 119,750, overlap the range of the other five explicit rounds (106,555 to 119,548), and its lowest round is above their lowest, so the cell is inside whichever way it is read. `/db` at 16 is bimodal again and is not judged.
