<!-- syscall-cost:begin -->
## Syscall cost

Not measured by `bench/run.mjs`, which carries this section over unchanged. `bash bench/syscall-cost/run.sh` times a syscall's entry and exit (`syscall(SYS_getppid)`) against the TCP work of a loopback request/response, pinned to one CPU; [bench/README.md](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/bench/README.md#syscall-cost) describes it and splits a keep-alive request's system time. 2026-10-30, `ce8f92ce`, Intel Xeon @ 2.10 GHz, KVM VM, Linux 6.18.44 (eIBRS, no KPTI), 7 interleaved rounds of 10^6 calls: entry and exit 106.0 ns (101.9–113.2), `recv` with nothing queued 173.7 ns, `setsockopt(TCP_QUICKACK, 0)` 179.7 ns, `getsockopt(TCP_INFO)` 283.0 ns, and 653 to 673 ns of TCP work per send or recv in a round.

For `bench/http1-server.ts` at 2.07 syscalls a request that is 0.22 µs of about 5 µs of system time, about 4%: under the 0.5 µs line, so io_uring is not worth building on this evidence. The reported workload ran on a Xeon Platinum 8260 VM on Linux 6.8, which this run did not measure.
<!-- syscall-cost:end -->

<!-- tls-handshake:begin -->
## TLS handshake

Not measured by `bench/run.mjs`, which carries this section over unchanged. `node bench/tls-handshake.mjs` times the server CPU of one TLS 1.3 handshake, `tests/link/net_interop_server` signing with its P-256 certificate, against a byte-identical copy of the server for the noise band and, with `--baseline REV`, against another rev's server. [bench/README.md](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/bench/README.md#tls-handshake) describes it, and [WP34 K5](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/docs/wp34-network-stack.md#k5-p-256-the-inversion--the-baseline) records its figures for the scalar inversion's baseline and [its result](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/docs/wp34-network-stack.md#k5-p-256-the-inversion--the-result).
<!-- tls-handshake:end -->

<!-- http-load:begin -->
## HTTP/2 and HTTP/3

Not measured by `bench/run.mjs`, which carries this section over unchanged. `node bench/h2load.mjs` drives `tests/link/net_interop_server` (`main serve`, pinned to CPU 0, 64 slots) with h2load on the other CPUs for HTTPS/1.1 and HTTP/2 over TLS, and with the server's own scripted QUIC client for HTTP/3, one build against another in interleaved rounds (a b, then b a). Run with one `--bin` it measures the build against itself, so the spread of the b / a ratios is the noise band.

```bash
build/seed/bin/nish tests/link/net_interop_server/main.ts -o build/interop.ir/ --link build/interop
node bench/h2load.mjs --bin build/interop --rounds 5
```

Measured 2026-11-12 with the interop server built from commit `747126db` (the main the branch started from, no source change) by the 0.38.0 seed, `build/seed/bin/nish ... --link build/interop`, profile `speed` (-O3 with LTO). Machine: a KVM guest, Intel Xeon @ 2.10 GHz, 4 vCPUs (one thread per core), 15 GB, Linux 6.18.44, h2load nghttp2 1.59.0 (Ubuntu 24.04), loopback. 5 rounds, 64 slots, 20,000 requests per round for the two h2load rows (16 clients; 8 streams each for HTTP/2, none for HTTPS/1.1) and 2,000 requests over one connection for HTTP/3.

| scenario | a req/s | b req/s | b / a (min–max) |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| HTTPS/1.1 `h2load --h1` c=16 | 88,771 | 85,270 | 0.911 (0.787–1.187) |
| HTTP/2 over TLS, `h2load` c=16 m=8 | 177,277 | 171,397 | 0.967 (0.704–1.173) |
| HTTP/3, `main h3-load` c=1, 2,000 requests, in process | 28,986 | 29,412 | 1.046 (0.920–1.566) |

A second identical run a few minutes later on the same VM gave 85,703 / 102,403 (1.137, 0.956–1.232), 155,584 / 166,240 (1.051, 0.805–1.213) and 30,303 / 30,303 (1.000, 0.881–1.241): the levels repeat to about 15%, so they are this machine's, not one run's luck.

a and b are the same binary, so the ratios are the noise band: about ±20 to 30% between the extremes of five rounds, and up to +57% on HTTP/3 in the first run. A difference between two builds smaller than that is not shown by five rounds; widen `--rounds` first.

**Against the table in [bench/README.md](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/bench/README.md)** (the same harness, 64 slots, 5 rounds: 47,354 / 74,411 / 13,245 req/s, HTTP/3 band 0.886–1.081). These figures are 1.9x, 2.4x and 2.2x higher. That table names no machine, compiler or commit, so the gap cannot be attributed here; what is shown is that it is not noise, since this VM reproduced its own levels twice. The likely causes are the host's CPU (this guest's cores are a different part and a shared host) and the build, and neither was varied. The two tables are each a ratio on one machine and are not to be read against each other; compare a build with another only within one table.

Not h2load's HTTP/3 number: Ubuntu's nghttp2-client is built without ngtcp2 and nghttp3, so the script falls back to `main h3-load`, the server and `client.ts`'s QUIC client in one process on CPU 0, one connection with its requests one after another. That figure is the two sides' work together and falls as the connection's streams pile up; it compares builds and is not a server capacity, nor comparable with an h2load HTTP/3 run.

What the cloud VM allows: the median requests per second of one shared-host guest, useful against another build measured the same way on the same machine, in the same session. They are not comparable with bare metal, another VM size or another h2load.
<!-- http-load:end -->

<!-- postgres-profile:begin -->
## PostgreSQL client: where the time goes

Not measured by `bench/run.mjs`, which carries this section over unchanged. `node bench/postgres/run.mjs --rounds 12` times the module against libpq ([bench/README.md](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/bench/README.md#postgresql) describes it); this section records what a profile of the module's side found, and the changes it suggested, none of which paid. 2026-10-27, main at 3a53579, four cores, PostgreSQL and libpq 18.6.

**Reading the tables.** A delta is a variant's median client CPU per query minus main's; + is slower. The noise band is main against its byte-identical copy in the same interleaved rounds, and each table is read against its own band: `list`'s measured 1.35 µs in the baseline and in the decoder table, and 2.06 µs in the buffer table, so 2.06 µs is the larger figure seen.

**The baseline.** `run.mjs --rounds 12`: 12 timed rounds after an untimed warm-up, the binaries interleaved within each round in an order that rotates by one each round. Client CPU per query, median (IQR):

| Workload | Nish | C | Nish − C | Noise band |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `pk` | 12.76 µs (0.38) | 15.22 µs (0.71) | −2.47 µs (−16.2 %) | 0.34 µs (2.7 %) |
| `list` | 53.88 µs (4.57) | 67.92 µs (4.95) | −14.03 µs (−20.7 %) | 1.35 µs (2.5 %) |
| `insert` | 32.13 µs (1.73) | 34.71 µs (3.36) | −2.59 µs (−8.1 %) | 0.67 µs (2.1 %) |

All three differences are outside the band. `run.mjs`'s own verdict also asks for a difference larger than both sides' IQRs, and that holds for `pk` and `list` but not for `insert`, whose −2.59 µs is inside C's 3.36 µs IQR, so the tool prints "inside the noise" for it.

**The profile.** `perf record` at 4,999 Hz over 200,000 `pk`, 20,000 `list` and 10,000 `insert` queries, from a speed build whose IR marks the module's decoders and the bench's fold functions `noinline`, so each shows its own self time. In a plain build `PgText.timestamp` is inlined into the bench's `Fold.timestamp` and the other decoders partly into `Bench.run`. Share of all samples, kernel included:

| | `pk` | `list` | `insert` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Kernel (socket, scheduler) | 91.2 % | 68.7 % | 92.9 % |
| The module, per query | 2.7 % | 10.5 % | 2.0 % |
| The bench's checksum fold (`Fold.*`, `Bench.*`) | 3.8 % | 17.9 % | 1.7 % |
| libc and the rest | 2.3 % | 2.8 % | 2.7 % |

`insert`'s module figure leaves out `sha256Compress` (1.4 %), SCRAM at connect. On `list` the module's functions are `PgText.timestamp` 2.4 %, `readRow` 2.2 %, `PgArrayWalker.next` 2.1 %, `PgText.int` 1.0 %, `dispatch` 0.9 %, `PgConnection.int` 0.4 %, and no other above 0.25 %. The bench's `Fold.bytes`, a byte-at-a-time checksum like `twin.c`'s, is 12.5 % by itself. On `pk` and `insert` no module function is above 0.7 %. Out of line, every decoder pays a call it does not pay in the shipped build, so 10.5 % of `list` (about 5.7 µs) overstates the module's share.

**Decoding, timed in isolation.** Each decoder called 20 million times on input that changes every call, with a printed checksum, pinned to one core; median of 9 runs, per call:

| | main | changed | the change |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `PgText.timestamp`, `2024-01-02 03:04:05.123456+00` | 18.8 ns | 20.6 ns | the 14 digits tested straight-line, an OR of `c - 48` and `57 - c`, in place of counting non-digits over 19 bytes |
| `PgText.int`, 7 digits | 10.3 ns | 9.1 ns | no overflow test per digit when there are at most 18 digits |
| `PgArrayWalker`, a 4-element `text[]` | 65.4 ns | 46.9 ns | an unquoted byte tested against the five refused bytes only when it is under 35, 92 or over 122 |

A `list` query decodes 20 rows of two timestamps, an int and an array, so all of its timestamp decoding is 40 × 18.8 ns, about 0.75 µs; removing it outright could not clear the 1.35 µs band. The walker change, the largest of the three, saves about 0.4 µs a query at 4 elements an array.

**Decoding, timed end to end.** The three changes and all three together, against main and its copy in 12 interleaved rounds (a fixed order within each round, no warm-up round):

| Workload | main | copy (the band) | timestamp | int | walker | all three |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `pk` | 13.54 µs (0.60) | −0.01 | +0.04 | −0.49 | −0.50 | −0.31 |
| `list` | 57.74 µs (7.37) | +1.35 | +2.33 | +1.21 | +0.59 | +1.42 |
| `insert` | 32.84 µs (5.32) | +1.29 | +0.20 | −0.01 | −1.47 | −1.14 |

On `list`, the only workload that decodes timestamps and arrays in bulk, every change measured slower than main, and none was faster by more than the band. The `pk` and `insert` cells outside their bands are noise: neither workload reads an array, yet the walker change moves both by the most. Its 18 ns a call is real, but at about 0.4 µs a `list` query it is about a quarter of the band, so no decoding change ships. Binary results were not tried: integer decoding (`PgText.int` and `PgConnection.int`) is about 1.4 % of `list` and under 0.7 % of the others, and the timestamp and `text[]` columns `list` reads would cost more to decode from binary than from text.

**The input buffer.** `strace -c` shows the module's 8 KiB first input buffer taking a `list` answer in two reads, 5,717 `recvfrom` and as many `epoll_wait` for 3,000 queries against libpq's 3,028. A 16 KiB first size, libpq's own, brings it to 3,028; 32 and 64 KiB to about 3,015. Timed against main in 12 interleaved rounds (a fixed order within each round, no warm-up round):

| Workload | main | main's copy (the band) | 16 KiB | 64 KiB |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `pk` | 12.85 µs (0.88) | +0.30 (2.3 %) | +0.09 (0.7 %) | +0.57 (4.4 %) |
| `list` | 52.97 µs (6.52) | +2.06 (3.9 %) | +2.02 (3.8 %) | +1.26 (2.4 %) |
| `insert` | 33.10 µs (2.04) | −0.74 (−2.2 %) | +0.86 (2.6 %) | −0.98 (−3.0 %) |

Three cells fall outside their band: `pk` at 64 KiB (+0.57, slower), `insert` at 16 KiB (+0.86, slower) and `insert` at 64 KiB (−0.98, faster). `list` at 16 KiB sits at its band. All are inside main's IQR, and the signs disagree between sizes and workloads. `list`, whose system calls the larger buffer halves, is not faster at either size, so the default stays 8 KiB.

**No system call left to remove.** `strace -c` over 2,000 `list` queries counts 1,990 `sendto` (at depth 16, one per answer that frees a slot), and the 16 KiB buffer above brings `recvfrom` and `epoll_wait` to about one each per query; `insert`, at depth 1, counts 2,007 of each. Each run makes two `epoll_ctl` calls in all.

The module already writes a query's Bind, Execute and Sync as one run of bytes that the caller sends in one call, and every query freed in a wakeup goes in that wakeup's one `sendto`, so fewer writes would need the caller to wait on more queries at once, which is not the module's to choose.

**Growing the buffer on demand, and everything together.** A buffer that starts at 8 KiB and doubles, up to 64 KiB, after a read fills it to its end, so only a connection that receives in bulk pays for the room: `strace` counts 2,023 `recvfrom` for 2,000 `list` queries against main's 3,829. Timed with the decoder changes too, in two runs of 12 rounds each after a warm-up round, the order rotating by one each round:

| Workload | Run | main | copy (the band) | growth | growth and the three decoders | the three decoders |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `pk` | 1 | 13.40 µs (0.88) | +0.01 | −0.11 | −0.04 | |
| `pk` | 2 | 12.65 µs (0.59) | +0.12 | +0.51 | +0.55 | +0.44 |
| `list` | 1 | 55.94 µs (3.83) | −0.22 | −0.64 | −1.48 | |
| `list` | 2 | 52.38 µs (2.98) | +0.11 | +0.31 | +2.63 | +1.80 |
| `insert` | 1 | 32.39 µs (1.97) | +0.37 | +0.58 | −1.17 | |
| `insert` | 2 | 31.26 µs (2.29) | +0.35 | −0.16 | +0.50 | +0.15 |

Run 1 put everything together 1.48 µs under main on `list`, outside that run's 0.22 µs band; run 2, the same binaries, put it 2.63 µs over. The copy's band, 0.1 to 0.4 µs in these runs, is about a tenth of how far the same binary's median moves between runs, so no single run's delta outside it is a result. Across the two runs no change is faster than main on every workload, and none is faster twice, so none ships, the growth included.

`readRow` was not changed: it is 2.2 % of `list` out of line, about 1.2 µs a query for 20 rows, and already reads each column's length without a call. Halving it would save less than the run-to-run spread above.
<!-- postgres-profile:end -->

<!-- db-clients:begin -->
## Database clients against C and Rust

Not measured by `bench/run.mjs`, which carries this section over unchanged. `nish/sqlite` and `nish/net/postgres` are timed against the C library each wraps or replaces and against the Rust crate most programs reach it through, on the multicore server challenge's schema. The workloads, the builds and the rules are in [bench/README.md](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/bench/README.md#sqlite) ([PostgreSQL](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/bench/README.md#postgresql)). A difference is claimed only when the median difference is larger than the noise band, the build against its byte-identical copy, and than the IQR of both sides; "seen" below means it is.

### SQLite: nish/sqlite, SQLite's C API and rusqlite

`node bench/sqlite/run.mjs --rounds 12`, the bench code at commit `f08a1674` (no later commit changes `std/sqlite.ts`, `runtime/sqlite/` or the bench's programs), on a four-core Intel Xeon virtual machine that reports 2.10 GHz, with Ubuntu clang 18.1.3, rustc 1.97.0, rusqlite 0.43.0 (libsqlite3-sys 0.40.0) and SQLite 3.54.1. rusqlite refuses a SQLite built `THREADSAFE=0`, so the "mt" columns are a second group on `THREADSAFE=2`, which a `--threads` build gets, with Rust linked to the same SQLite object as C mt. Median ns per call (IQR):

| Workload | Nish | C | Nish mt | C mt | Rust mt (rusqlite) | Noise band |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `lookup` | 6,005 (195) | 5,987 (294) | 6,335 (370) | 6,098 (253) | 6,193 (180) | 32 |
| `feed` | 20,894 (2,090) | 21,353 (1,616) | 22,222 (5,219) | 20,706 (702) | 22,449 (2,743) | 1,136 |
| `insert` | 20,254 (1,203) | 20,221 (1,534) | 21,272 (2,417) | 20,764 (912) | 20,744 (2,833) | 431 |
| `insert-txn` | 5,983 (885) | 5,981 (456) | 6,233 (1,317) | 6,084 (169) | 6,412 (689) | 88 |

No difference in this table is seen: each is inside the IQR of at least one of the two sides it compares. SQLite's own work dominates every call. The one cost a finer instrument has found is on `feed`, 20 rows of five columns a call: under callgrind the build runs 4.2 % more instructions a call than C, about 7,800 of 186,300, with the adapter fix of commit `0e40dff2` in (bench/README.md has the command).

### PostgreSQL: nish/net/postgres, libpq and tokio-postgres

`node bench/postgres/run.mjs --rounds 12`, the bench code and `std/` at commit `df3f1023`, on a four-core Intel Xeon virtual machine that reports 2.10 GHz, with Ubuntu clang 18.1.3, rustc 1.97.0, tokio-postgres 0.7.18 on tokio 1.54.1's current-thread runtime, and PostgreSQL and libpq 18.7, the server on the same machine. `pk` is 50,000 primary-key reads at 16 in flight, `list` 3,000 reads of 20 rows at 16 in flight, `insert` 5,000 inserts one at a time. Client CPU in µs per query, min / median (IQR); system time and qps are medians:

| Workload | Nish | C (libpq) | Rust (tokio-postgres) | Noise band | System, Nish / C / Rust | qps, Nish / C / Rust |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `pk` | 6.50 / 7.06 (0.42) | 8.00 / 8.26 (0.32) | 9.11 / 9.32 (0.47) | 0.41 | 5.76 / 5.38 / 5.06 | 40,175 / 40,266 / 41,131 |
| `list` | 45.84 / 49.90 (1.68) | 61.11 / 63.64 (2.74) | 59.58 / 63.39 (4.54) | 0.05 | 26.86 / 25.81 / 22.47 | 409 / 403 / 409 |
| `insert` | 14.64 / 18.21 (2.14) | 15.95 / 19.13 (2.37) | 18.68 / 21.37 (1.88) | 1.58 | 16.53 / 17.08 / 16.59 | 4,687 / 4,603 / 4,585 |

| Workload | Nish − C | Rust − C | Nish − Rust |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `pk` | −1.20 (−14.6 %), seen | +1.06 (+12.9 %), seen | −2.26 (−24.3 %), seen |
| `list` | −13.75 (−21.6 %), seen | −0.25 (−0.4 %), inside the noise | −13.49 (−21.3 %), seen |
| `insert` | −0.92 (−4.8 %), inside the noise | +2.24 (+11.7 %), inside the noise | −3.16 (−14.8 %), seen |

On this run the module costs the client less CPU than tokio-postgres on all three workloads, and less than libpq on `pk` and `list`; on `insert` its difference from libpq is inside the noise. tokio-postgres costs more than libpq on `pk`; on `list` and `insert` the difference is inside the noise. qps is within 2.4 % across the three sides on every row, so client CPU is not what limits throughput at these depths. On `pk` and `insert` most of each side's CPU is system time, which no client's library code spends; on `list`, where 20 rows are decoded a query, it is about half of the module's CPU and less than half of libpq's and tokio-postgres's.

### The optimisation pass behind the PostgreSQL figures

Commit `df3f1023` changed two things in `std/net/postgres.ts`. `PgText.int` reads a column of at most 18 bytes as two runs of digits, each below 10^9, so the bounds prover sees that nothing there can wrap and the digit loop has no overflow test. `PgConnection.reserve` keeps only its length test and calls out to grow, so the test every put makes inlines. The module's unproven panic sites under `--emit-panics` stayed at 591. Instructions per operation under callgrind, from `node bench/pg-decode/run.mjs --callgrind --base HEAD`, run with the change uncommitted on `d81c00c1`, so `base` is `std/` at `d81c00c1`. A byte-identical copy of the build agreed with it to 0.0 % on every case. The kernels are [bench/pg-decode/main.ts](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/bench/pg-decode/main.ts)'s: `rows` decodes a binary-format answer of n rows, `text` the same rows in the text format, `pool` the binary group through a one-slot `PgPool`, and `params` encodes a text-format Bind of n keys and n ids:

| Case | `d81c00c1` | `df3f1023` | Change |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| `rows 1` (214 B) | 3,662 | 3,350 | −8.5 % |
| `rows 20` (2,892 B) | 26,911 | 26,599 | −1.2 % |
| `rows 100` (14,019 B) | 123,159 | 122,847 | −0.3 % |
| `text 1` (218 B) | 4,013 | 3,721 | −7.3 % |
| `text 20` (2,464 B) | 40,694 | 39,909 | −1.9 % |
| `text 100` (12,153 B) | 198,368 | 195,145 | −1.6 % |
| `pool 1` (214 B) | 4,119 | 3,807 | −7.6 % |
| `pool 20` (2,892 B) | 28,261 | 27,949 | −1.1 % |
| `pool 100` (14,019 B) | 128,316 | 128,004 | −0.2 % |
| `params 1` (104 B) | 3,923 | 3,447 | −12.1 % |
| `params 20` (1,051 B) | 29,136 | 26,550 | −8.9 % |
| `params 100` (5,179 B) | 138,200 | 126,734 | −8.3 % |

The clock cannot resolve these changes in the end-to-end figures above, so callgrind is the evidence for them. The same bench at `5aee9afb`'s `std/`, on the same kind of machine in an earlier session, put C's own `pk` median at 7.80 µs against 8.26 µs here: an unchanged binary moved 5.9 % between runs. Tried and dropped in the same pass: rewrites of `PgText.timestamp`, which ran more instructions; a comma scan in `PgArrayWalker`, a digit count in `putDecimal` and an inlined `getI32BE`, none of which gave a measured win; and a digit loop that wraps instead of testing, which needs `nish:unsafe` and so would make every program that uses the module reach `unsafe`.
<!-- db-clients:end -->

<!-- unix-ipc:begin -->
## Unix sockets against loopback TCP

Not measured by `bench/run.mjs`, which carries this section over unchanged; recorded by hand. `node bench/unix-ipc.mjs` times an echo between two processes over loopback TCP (TCP_NODELAY), a Unix stream socketpair and a seqpacket socketpair, and `node bench/postgres/run.mjs --unix` the module's `SELECT 1` and a 100-row read over TCP and over the server's Unix socket; [bench/README.md](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/bench/README.md#unix-sockets-against-loopback-tcp) describes both and has the full tables. 2026-10-31, the bench code as of `add5bcf` (the commits after it reformat it and add error handling outside the timed loops; what is measured is unchanged), Intel Xeon @ 2.80 GHz, 4 CPUs, KVM VM, Linux 6.18.44, PostgreSQL 18.6 (pgdg), 12 interleaved rounds after a warm-up.

The noise band is the same transport run twice in every round (unix-ipc's run a against run b, postgres's build against its byte-identical copy); a difference counts only outside it and both sides' IQRs.

| | tcp | unix | unix − tcp | noise band | verdict |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| echo 64 B, round trip median | 51.16 µs | 42.59 µs (stream) | −8.56 µs (−16.7 %) | 1.29 µs | outside the noise |
| echo 64 B, round trip p99 | 110.93 µs | 99.08 µs (stream) | −11.85 µs (−10.7 %) | 2.34 µs | inside TCP's IQR (17.86): noise |
| echo 64 B, client CPU/trip | 27.25 µs | 21.49 µs (stream) | −5.76 µs (−21.2 %) | 0.76 µs | outside the noise |
| echo 64 B, client CPU/trip | 27.25 µs | 22.13 µs (seqpacket) | −5.12 µs (−18.8 %) | 0.76 µs | outside the noise |
| echo 64 B, peer CPU/trip | 27.12 µs | 20.52 µs (stream) | −6.61 µs (−24.4 %) | 1.22 µs | outside the noise |
| echo 64 B, peer CPU/trip | 27.12 µs | 21.25 µs (seqpacket) | −5.87 µs (−21.6 %) | 1.22 µs | outside the noise |
| echo 4 KB, round trip median | 53.07 µs | 47.78 µs (stream) | −5.29 µs (−10.0 %) | 0.93 µs | outside the noise |
| echo 4 KB, client CPU/trip | 35.54 µs | 31.74 µs (stream) | −3.80 µs (−10.7 %) | 0.95 µs | outside the noise |
| echo 4 KB, client CPU/trip | 35.54 µs | 30.27 µs (seqpacket) | −5.27 µs (−14.8 %) | 0.95 µs | outside the noise |
| echo 4 KB, peer CPU/trip | 28.15 µs | 24.01 µs (stream) | −4.14 µs (−14.7 %) | 0.67 µs | outside the noise |
| echo 4 KB, peer CPU/trip | 28.15 µs | 23.24 µs (seqpacket) | −4.91 µs (−17.4 %) | 0.67 µs | outside the noise |
| `SELECT 1`, wall/query | 66.13 µs | 56.60 µs | −9.53 µs (−14.4 %) | 1.38 µs | outside the noise |
| `SELECT 1`, client CPU/query | 28.13 µs | 21.95 µs | −6.19 µs (−22.0 %) | 0.40 µs | outside the noise |
| `SELECT 1`, server CPU/query | 37.39 µs | 30.71 µs | −6.67 µs (−17.9 %) | 1.21 µs | outside the noise |
| 100 rows, wall/query | 191.59 µs | 173.30 µs | −18.30 µs (−9.5 %) | 1.85 µs | outside the noise, by 2 µs over TCP's IQR (16.27) |
| 100 rows, client CPU/query | 70.59 µs | 61.00 µs | −9.59 µs (−13.6 %) | 1.52 µs | outside the noise |
| 100 rows, server CPU/query | 159.13 µs | 141.08 µs | −18.06 µs (−11.3 %) | 0.24 µs | outside the noise |

The echo client's CPU per trip includes making each message and checking the reply byte by byte inside its timed loop, a cost the same on every transport that the peer, echoing only, does not pay: it is why the client's figure is above the peer's at 4 KB and its percentages the smaller; the peer's row is the transport alone.

Seqpacket is within the noise of stream in every figure (64 B median 44.61 µs, 4 KB 47.11 µs); its CPU rows, from the same rounds, are in the table beside stream's. The 4 KB p99 is inside the noise for both Unix kinds. A 3-round run on the same box put the bands 1.3 to 8 times wider (64 B median 2.18 µs, 4 KB median 7.43 µs) and found the 4 KB round trip inside the noise; the 12-round figures above are the record.
<!-- unix-ipc:end -->

<!-- regex:begin -->
## Regular expressions

Not measured by `bench/run.mjs`, which carries this section over unchanged. `node bench/regex/measure.mjs --before <nish> --rounds 24 --size` times `nish/regex` over a 64 KB corpus of mixed ASCII and non-ASCII lines, against a byte-identical copy of the binary for the noise band and against the same program built with another compiler's `std/` ([bench/README.md](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/bench/README.md#regular-expressions) describes it). 2026-10-30: this branch against `main` at 384371b, before property escapes and non-ASCII folding, one run of 24 interleaved rounds, four cores, x86-64.

The cases without the Unicode features. Timings this short move by more than any difference between the two builds, so the verdict is the instructions a pass retires, counted with `valgrind --tool=cachegrind` as the difference between a run of 40 passes and one of 20 (no setup). The time ratio is main's over this branch's, so above 1 means this branch is faster.

| Case | Pattern | Instructions a pass, main | Branch / main | Median, main | Median, branch | Time ratio (min / median) | Noise band (median, main / branch) |
| --- | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `literal` | `needle` | 2,287,435 | 1.000x | 8.3 µs | 8.3 µs | 0.999x / 0.997x | 0.1% / 0.7% |
| `class` | `[0-9]+` | 6,541,291 | 1.000x | 34.3 µs | 30.7 µs | 1.094x / 1.119x | 3.2% / 0.1% |
| `alternation` | `fox\|dog\|needle\|World` | 9,476,271 | 1.000x | 60.7 µs | 59.9 µs | 1.035x / 1.014x | 3.0% / 0.5% |
| `group` | `(?:[a-z]+ ){2}[a-z]+` | 34,539,670 | 1.000x | 269.8 µs | 257.4 µs | 1.010x / 1.048x | 1.9% / 4.8% |
| `anchored` | `^[A-Z][a-z]+` (`m`) | 5,661,967 | 0.992x | 21.1 µs | 20.1 µs | 0.997x / 1.050x | 0.3% / 3.4% |
| `icase-ascii` | `hello world\|quick` (`i`) | 5,267,653 | 1.000x | 25.0 µs | 24.2 µs | 0.976x / 1.033x | 0.3% / 1.6% |
| `icase-class` | `[A-Za-z]+` (`i`) | 62,588,232 | 1.000x | 438.0 µs | 432.4 µs | 1.049x / 1.013x | 4.0% / 4.3% |
| `exec` | `(\w+)@(\w+)\.(com\|org)` | 169,456,374 | 0.926x | 515.8 µs | 530.7 µs | 1.094x / 0.972x | 10.3% / 11.3% |

The instruction columns are counted against `main` at 1391cf8, whose `src/` this branch's is. Six cases retire main's instructions to within 0.01%. `class`, `alternation` and `icase-class` were 0.5% over until the DFA's two context masks, which gained a sixth slot saying whether U+017F and U+212A are word characters, were sized by a named constant again: sized `CTX_KINDS + 1`, they were no longer a constant length, so they left the `Regex` object for the heap and `dfaForward` paid a pointer load per read.

`anchored` was 2.6% over until a context read under `i` stopped testing U+017F and U+212A on ASCII bytes. `exec` retires 7.4% fewer: all of it is in the Pike VM's `addThread`, whose source is main's, where the loops that copy capture slots compile to fewer instructions around the inlined `assertHolds`; no algorithm changed, and its time ratio is inside the band.

Compiling alone: a pass compiles the case's patterns once, 10,000 passes a run. Instructions are per compile, counted the same way between 4,000 and 2,000 passes.

| Case | Patterns | Instructions, main | Branch / main | Median, main | Median, branch | Time ratio (min / median) | Noise band (median, main / branch) |
| --- | --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `compile-class` | a large class with non-ASCII letters | 26,785 | 0.990x | 5.0 µs | 5.2 µs | 1.006x / 0.979x | 0.8% / 1.0% |
| `compile-icase-class` | the same class under `i` | 27,376 | 5.67x | 5.0 µs | 17.1 µs | 0.321x / 0.291x | 3.9% / 9.5% |
| `compile-nonspace-icase` | `\S+` (`i`) | 16,891 | 0.967x | 3.6 µs | 3.8 µs | 1.016x / 0.944x | 1.3% / 5.1% |
| `compile-icase-literal` | `é` (`i`) | 3,885 | 1.53x | 1.9 µs | 2.1 µs | 0.951x / 0.893x | 0.8% / 4.7% |
| `compile-many` | ten small patterns | 274,390 | 1.002x | 47.2 µs | 46.7 µs | 0.977x / 1.011x | 2.5% / 1.0% |

The two folding rows are the real cost, and they are work main does not do: main folds no letter outside ASCII. A compile reads the folding table in place, so nothing is decoded ahead of time and nothing is kept between compiles. An index of every sixteenth folding code point finds a letter's place, and a flag on two-letter orbits saves the second lookup. The large class costs about 12 µs more than main. A single `é` costs about 0.2 µs. A pattern that folds nothing above ASCII never reads the table. `\S`, `\D` and `\W` under `i` don't either, being closed under folding already.

The three cases with the Unicode features. Main refuses `\p{…}` and folds no non-ASCII letter, so these time this branch alone:

| Case | Pattern | Median | Noise band (median) |
| --- | --- | ---: | --- |
| `property` | `\p{L}+` | 601.7 µs | 0.0% |
| `script` | `\p{Script=Greek}+` | 354.5 µs | 2.0% |
| `icase-unicode` | `σοφία\|école\|straße\|über` (`i`) | 358.2 µs | 5.6% |

Linked size of `bench/regex/size.ts`, which imports `nish/regex` and compiles `[0-9]+`: 92,824 bytes from main and 177,448 from this branch. The 84,624 bytes are mostly the Unicode tables, about 70 KB of packed strings, plus the code that reads them. `regexCompile`'s parser reaches them, so they are linked whether or not a pattern names a property.
<!-- regex:end -->

## HTTP/1.1 request parser

Not measured by `bench/run.mjs`, which carries this section over unchanged. `node bench/http1-parse/run.mjs` parses one request a head with the live parser (`new`), main's parser frozen beside it (`old`) and a second frozen copy (`old-copy`, the noise band); [bench/README.md](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/bench/README.md#the-http11-request-parser) describes it and has the commands for the Cascade Lake VM. Instructions a head are callgrind's, the difference between 12,000 and 2,000 iterations over 10,000.

| | |
| --- | --- |
| Date | 2026-11-03 |
| nish | this branch against `main` at 96a38347, `--profile speed` |
| CPU | Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.80GHz, 4 logical cores (sse2, avx2, bmi2, avx512bw); baseline x86-64, u8x16 on SSE2 |
| Runs | 24 interleaved rounds of 3 parsers a head; median (min–max) ns a head |
| Command | `npm run build && node bench/http1-parse/run.mjs` |

| head | old | old-copy (band) | new | new against old / old-copy | instructions a head | instructions a byte |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| loadgen GET, 337 bytes | 406.9 | 420.3 (±3.3%) | 344.9 (316.1–477.2) | −15.2% / −17.9% | 5,617 → 4,769 | 16.7 → 14.2 |
| loadgen POST, 447 bytes | 655.0 | 591.8 (±9.6%) | 483.8 (439.3–688.6) | −26.1% / −18.2% | 7,761 → 6,483 | 17.4 → 14.5 |
| browser GET, 1,028 bytes | 1,299.7 | 1,373.5 (±5.7%) | 1,024.4 (936.9–1,531.5) | −21.2% / −25.4% | 16,335 → 13,667 | 15.9 → 13.3 |

Under `--cpu x86-64-v3`: −18.6%, −17.1%, −24.1% (bands ±2.6%, ±0.4%, ±6.4%). The steps, in instructions a head (loadgen GET / POST / browser GET), each kept because it moved all three: the line scan jumps to the first control byte of a 16-byte block instead of finishing it a byte at a time (5,617/7,761/16,335 → 5,399/7,422/15,757); a branch-free token check against a 128-bit set (→ 5,182/7,098/14,962); a kept field name lowercased while it is scanned in `head` rather than in a second pass (→ 5,113/6,987/14,627); CRLF taken in one step (→ 4,776/6,492/13,685). Results are checked identical to main's parser by `tests/link/net_http1_parse_diff`.

## Http1Connection per request, hot, cold, idle and DRAM-cold

Not measured by `bench/run.mjs`, which carries this section over unchanged. `node bench/http1-conn/run.mjs` times `Http1Connection` and the codec under it per request, with no socket, against `main`'s `std/` (`old`) and a byte-identical copy of that build (`old′`, the noise band); [bench/http1-conn/README.md](https://github.com/amritk/nish/blob/main/bench/http1-conn/README.md) describes the six shapes, the working set against the caches, the RSS mode and `misses.sh`.

| | |
| --- | --- |
| Date | 2026-11-06 |
| nish | the hot-first `Http1Parser` layout against `main` at 98c4f9a7, `--profile speed` |
| CPU | Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.30GHz, 4 cores (L1d 48 KiB and L2 2 MiB a core, L3 504 MiB), pinned to core 1 |
| Runs | 15 interleaved rounds, each the fastest of 5 batches; 200,000 requests a batch, 20,000 in the `-dram` shapes, which sweep a 1 GiB eviction ring between requests; cold, idle and direct over 10,000 connections (about 10 MiB, inside L3) |
| Command | `node bench/http1-conn/run.mjs --connections 10000 --rounds 15 --base 98c4f9a7`, then `bash bench/http1-conn/misses.sh` |

| shape | old | old′ | new |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| hot | 323.0 (308.1–329.9) | 324.4 (310.1–332.1) 1.004 | 320.0 (305.8–333.6) 0.990 |
| cold | 353.3 (334.5–379.9) | 356.9 (339.7–382.9) 1.010 | 341.6 (332.1–375.0) 0.967 |
| idle | 446.5 (425.9–472.9) | 439.6 (424.4–452.9) 0.984 | 441.7 (421.0–444.2) 0.989 |
| direct | 434.3 (415.2–442.4) | 435.8 (415.7–440.2) 1.004 | 434.7 (414.8–439.7) 1.001 |
| idle-dram | 672.0 (650.3–735.5) | 668.5 (632.8–701.6) 0.995 | 650.7 (620.0–677.0) 0.968 |
| direct-dram | 660.2 (643.8–715.8) | 666.5 (641.2–715.5) 1.010 | 649.1 (626.1–666.3) 0.983 |
| peak RSS per idle connection, bytes | 1026 (1025–1026) | 1026 (1025–1026) | 1026 (1025–1026) |

ns per request, median (min–max) and ratio of medians to `old`. `idle` shows no wall-time gain outside noise. In `idle-dram` the median is 3.2% under `old` against `old′`'s 0.5%, but the ranges overlap.

What the layout does measurably reduce is lines touched. Under cachegrind, an idle request misses D1 10.95 times on `main` and 9.01 on this layout, and LL 0 times on both, because the pool fits in LL. The count is the difference between 100,000 and 0 requests over 10,000 connections. In `idle-dram`, with the eviction sweep's own misses left out, it misses LL 10.94 times on `main` and 9.00 on this layout. The saving is in `Http1Connection.feed` (2.38 to 0.75 read misses) and in the write misses of `unpark`, `http1Next` and `Http1Connection.next`; `http1Next` and `inputRoom` take on 0.44 and 0.25 read misses. Reading straight into the lent `parser.buf` (`direct`) saves no misses.

## A minimal GET on Http1Server

Not measured by `bench/run.mjs`, which carries this section over unchanged. This section breaks down one keep-alive `GET` answered with a 200 and a small JSON body: where its instructions go, its wall-clock time, the bytes a connection holds, and the server CPU and memory at 18,000 keep-alive connections. It then bounds what a lean request path could save (#1760). `std/`, `src/` and `runtime/` are `main` at 4d0faa4b throughout. Each table names the bench code that produced it: either `main` at 4d0faa4b with a path, or "this PR's bench code", the files this section's PR adds or changes under `bench/`.

| | |
| --- | --- |
| Date | 2026-11-18 |
| nish | `main` at 4d0faa4b, `--profile speed`, built `--cpu x86-64` |
| CPU | Intel(R) Xeon(R) Processor @ 2.10GHz, 4 cores (L1d 48 KiB and L2 2 MiB a core, L3 260 MiB), 15.7 GiB |
| Runs | callgrind and cachegrind with `NISH_SIMD=base` and glibc's string functions pinned by `GLIBC_TUNABLES`, 1,000 iterations less 500, over 500; wall clock in 12 interleaved rounds against a byte-identical copy (`bench/http1-request`), and in 15 rounds (7 for the stages) against `old` and `old′` (`bench/http1-conn`); `bench/handler-ring` in 5 interleaved rounds |
| Command | `node bench/http1-request/breakdown.mjs`; `node bench/http1-request/run.mjs --instructions`; `node bench/http1-request/run.mjs --case get --case short --case fields0 --case fields4 --case fields10`; `node bench/http1-conn/run.mjs --shape hot,cold,idle-dram,direct-dram --connections 18000 --rounds 15 --base origin/main`; `node bench/http1-conn/run.mjs --shape hot,idle --connections 1 --rounds 15 --rss 0 --base origin/main`; `node bench/http1-conn/run.mjs --shape cold,idle-dram,direct-dram,stages --connections 18000 --rounds 7 --base origin/main` (origin/main at 4d0faa4b in each); `build/nish bench/http1-conn/bytes.ts --link build/bench/http1-conn/bytes && build/bench/http1-conn/bytes`; `bash bench/handler-ring/run.sh --nodb --conns 18000 --designs "ring epoll1 epoll epoll-copy" --server-cpus 0`; `bash bench/handler-ring/run.sh --nodb --conns 18000 --designs "h1 h1-copy epoll1" --rounds 5` |

The request shapes are cases of `bench/http1-request` (this PR's bench code, `bench/http1-request/main.ts`):

- `get` is 350 bytes: `GET /json` with `Host: api.example.com`, `User-Agent`, `Accept: application/json`, and a 221-byte HS256 Bearer `Authorization`. The program reads none of the last three.
- `short` is 37 bytes: `GET / HTTP/1.1` and `Host: example.com`.
- `fields0`, `fields4` and `fields10` are `GET /json` with `Host: example.com` and 0, 4 or 10 `X-Pad-k: value-k` fields. They are 41, 113 and 221 bytes, and give the cost of each unread field.

Each case is answered `200` with `Content-Type: application/json` and a JSON body written by the handler. Every request has the same status and fields as the one before, so the response head takes the kept-head path.

### Instructions a request, by component

Every instruction is given to the source function its debug line sits in, not the function callgrind names, because the optimiser inlines most of `std/net` into its callers. `bench/http1-request/breakdown.mjs` does this over `bench/http1-request/main.ts`, both this PR's bench code. It prints a row per function at 1 Ir or more a request; the grouping into the components below is done by hand from those rows. The totals are of the `-g` build under callgrind. Under cachegrind, `run.mjs --instructions` counts a build without debug info at 6,116, 3,490, 3,512, 5,309 and 8,094, within 1.8% of the totals below.

| component | `get` | `short` | `fields0` | `fields4` | `fields10` |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| head parse: request line, `next`, end of head (the rest of `http1-codec`) | 1,303.5 | 842.6 | 864.7 | 1,065.9 | 1,389.3 |
| head parse: line scan (`http1Section` less line 1880) | 952 | 260 | 260 | 692 | 1,363 |
| header spans: field lines (`http1FieldLine`, `http1LowerName`, `http1NonTokenLanes`, `http1SpanSet`, `http1FramingField`, `http1HeadIs`, `http1HeadIsVector`, `headerIndex`) | 1,423 | 346 | 346 | 1,418 | 3,026 |
| header spans: head copy (`http1Section` line 1880) | 252 | 24 | 24 | 72 | 156 |
| `memcpy` | 136 | 57 | 57 | 65 | 112 |
| Host check (`std/net/url.ts`) | 448 | 361 | 361 | 361 | 361 |
| Date (`HttpDate.current` 14, `HttpDate.formats` 1) | 15 | 15 | 15 | 15 | 15 |
| response head, kept path | 451 | 451 | 451 | 451 | 451 |
| sendBody: `write` and `end` | 86 | 86 | 86 | 86 | 86 |
| connection, the rest | 323 | 323 | 323 | 323 | 323 |
| JSON writer (the handler) | 494 | 494 | 494 | 494 | 494 |
| bench harness | 176 | 176 | 176 | 176 | 176 |
| total | 6,059.6 | 3,435.6 | 3,457.8 | 5,219.0 | 7,952.3 |

Instructions a request (Ir), callgrind; this PR's bench code (`bench/http1-request/main.ts`, `bench/http1-request/breakdown.mjs`).

- **Header spans.** On `get`, the field lines are `http1FieldLine` 540, `http1HeadIs` 353, `http1NonTokenLanes` 194, `http1LowerName` (the lowercasing) 176, `headerIndex` 71, `http1SpanSet` (the span stores) 40, `http1FramingField` 29 and `http1HeadIsVector` 20.
- **Unread fields.** From `fields0` to `fields10`, each unread field costs 449.5 Ir. That is 268 Ir of field lines (`http1FieldLine` 133, `http1LowerName` 44, `http1NonTokenLanes` 39, `headerIndex` 17, `http1SpanSet` 10, `http1HeadIsVector` 10, `http1FramingField` 8, `http1HeadIs` 7), 110 of line scan, 13 of head copy, 5.5 of `memcpy` and 52 in the rest of the codec. Cachegrind's slope is 458.2.
- **Response head, kept path.** Its 451 Ir are `http1ServerKept` 99, `sameHead` 70, `http1ServerSame8` 64, `respond` 110, `copyBytes` 49, `bodyRoom` 28, `http1ServerCopy` 20 and `begin` 11. `sameHead` compares the kept head, its Date included, and on a match `copyBytes` copies the kept head whole. `http1WriteResponseHead` is not in the steady-state profile: it runs only when the status, the fields or the Date change.
- **Connection, the rest.** This is `inputRoom` 64, `nextRequest` 53, `next` 27, `room` 26, `acquire` 28, `OutputPool.take` 27, `release` 16, and 82 Ir of smaller rows. The per-request state reset is `http1StartRequest` 22 plus `Http1Connection.nextRequest` 53, 75 Ir.
- **Date.** A lean path keeps Date: RFC 9110 §6.6.1 says an origin server with a clock MUST send Date in its 2xx, 3xx and 4xx responses. It costs 15 Ir a request here. `HttpDate.current` returns the pinned time, or else the time held by `hold()`, or else reads the clock. `Http1Server` holds once per readable event (`std/net/http1-server.ts`, lines 2574 and 2621). `bench/http1-request` and `bench/http1-conn` both pin the date, so neither times a clock read inside `HttpDate`. At 30,000 requests a second over 18,000 connections a readable event mostly carries one request, so the read can be once a request: at the 28.2 ns of the `clock read` row below, which is the bench's own `monotonicNanos` read, that is up to about 28 ns a request, and a lean path pays it too. The 29-byte Date copy happens only in `http1WriteResponseHead`, so the kept-head path does not make it.

### Wall clock

`node bench/http1-request/run.mjs` (this PR's bench code, `bench/http1-request/`), 12 rounds of 2 columns a case. Each figure is ns a request, median (min–max). The band is the byte-identical copy's median against its build's.

| case | ns | copy | band | ns per Ir | copy ns per Ir |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| `short` | 302.2 (262.1–409.9) | 299.5 (273.0–390.5) | ±0.9% | 0.088 | 0.087 |
| `get` | 466.9 (447.9–655.0) | 489.3 (449.9–602.9) | ±4.8% | 0.077 | 0.081 |
| `fields0` | 286.6 (271.4–436.9) | 304.1 (264.9–364.6) | ±6.1% | 0.083 | 0.088 |
| `fields4` | 501.2 (392.6–738.7) | 530.8 (389.5–629.9) | ±5.9% | 0.096 | 0.102 |
| `fields10` | 628.6 (599.3–843.1) | 687.3 (601.6–840.8) | ±9.3% | 0.079 | 0.086 |

The ns-per-Ir columns divide each median by the callgrind total above. The per-field slope by wall clock is noisy: 34.2 ns a field from `fields0` to `fields10`, and 53.7 from `fields0` to `fields4`. `get` less `fields0` is 180.3 ns at the medians.

`node bench/http1-conn/run.mjs` (`main` at 4d0faa4b, `bench/http1-conn/main.ts` and `run.mjs`) times `Http1Connection` without a socket on the GET of about 350 bytes. Each round is the fastest of 5 batches of 200,000 requests, pinned to core 1. The shapes are:

- `hot`: one connection, the GET alone, every line in cache.
- `cold`: the GET over 18,000 connections visited round-robin. 18,000 connections hold about 21 MB by the byte counts below, which fits in this box's 260 MiB L3 but not its 2 MiB L2, so `cold` reaches L3 but not DRAM.
- `idle`: an `Http1Server`-shaped pool with `idleInput` on: each request is read through the server's scratch buffer, the connection lends its input and head buffers back when it parks, and the requests alternate the GET with a POST of about 550 bytes.
- `idle-dram` and `direct-dram`: `idle` over 18,000 connections, and its variant that reads into the lent input buffer with no scratch copy, run 20,000 a batch with a 1 GiB eviction ring swept between requests, so every request finds its connection in DRAM. Only the requests are timed, each between two clock reads.

`old` is `main`'s `std/` and `old′` a byte-identical copy of its build. `new` is the working tree, whose `std/` is the same 4d0faa4b, so all three columns are one code and their spread is noise.

| shape | old | old′ | new |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| hot, 1 connection | 378.6 (374.1–395.0) | 377.2 (373.1–394.4) 0.996 | 375.8 (373.5–396.0) 0.993 |
| cold, 18,000 connections | 448.5 (404.0–626.0) | 437.2 (401.1–626.5) 0.975 | 442.8 (404.5–659.9) 0.987 |
| idle-dram, 18,000 connections | 732.6 (687.5–794.4) | 733.8 (688.0–859.3) 1.002 | 735.4 (697.3–801.7) 1.004 |
| direct-dram, 18,000 connections | 703.0 (669.3–886.0) | 687.0 (655.1–756.6) 0.977 | 688.2 (661.4–786.6) 0.979 |

15 rounds; ns per request, median (min–max) and ratio of medians to `old`.

| shape | old | old′ | new |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| hot, 1 connection | 386.9 (376.8–475.0) | 388.2 (378.3–480.1) 1.003 | 386.5 (379.1–460.5) 0.999 |

| stage | old | old′ | new |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| recv | 57.8 (56.2–60.8) | 59.1 (54.3–68.3) | 59.0 (56.2–62.2) |
